\chapter{Problems Related to Volume II}
\label{ch:companion-ii}

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{Main-text correspondence.}\quad
This Part accompanies \emph{Volume II: Real Analysis and Topological Foundations}. Problems are grouped by
mathematical theme rather than by reproducing the chapter sequence of the main text.
Each section gives the corresponding main-text chapter range for further reading.

\section{Metric and Topological Foundations}

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{Related material.}\quad  Volume II, Chapters \texttt{II/01--II/07}.

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0006]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0006}
\par\noindent (a)\quad Show that $\mathcal{S}$ is a vector subspace of
	$\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^n)$.  Show that if
	$\{\phi_j\}_{j=1}^\infty$ is a sequence of rapidly
	decreasing functions which tends to zero in $\mathcal{S}$,
	then $\phi_j \to 0$ in $\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^n)$.

	\par\noindent (b)\quad Show that $\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is a vector subspace
	of $\mathcal{S}$.  Show that if $\{\phi_j\}_{j=1}^\infty$
	is a sequence of compactly supported functions which tends
	to zero in $\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^n)$, then
	$\phi_j \to 0$ in $\mathcal{S}$.

	\par\noindent (c)\quad Give an example of a sequence
	$\{\phi_j\}_{j=1}^\infty \subset C_0^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$
	such that

		\par\noindent (i)\quad $\phi_j \to 0$ in $\mathcal{S}$, but
		$\{\phi_j\}$ has no limit in
		$\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^n)$;
		\par\noindent (ii)\quad $\phi_j \to 0$ in $\mathcal{E}(\mathbb{R}^n)$,
		but $\{\phi_j\}$ has no limit in $\mathcal{S}$.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0008]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0008}
\renewcommand\labelenumi{(\alph{enumi})}
\par\noindent\textbullet\quad If $U_1,U_2$ are open, then $U_1\cup U_2$ and $U_1\cap U_2$ are open.
\par\noindent\textbullet\quad If $E_1,E_2$ are closed, then $E_1\cup E_2$ and $E_1\cap E_2$ are closed.
\par\noindent\textbullet\quad For any collection $\mathcal U$ of open sets: $\bigcup\mathcal U$ is open; $\bigcap\mathcal U$ need not be open (e.g.\ $\cap_{n\ge1}(-1/n,1/n)=\{0\}$).
For closed sets: arbitrary intersections are closed, finite unions are closed, infinite unions need not be closed.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0013]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0013}
Let $C[0,1]$ be the metric space of continuous functions $f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}$ with the distance
\[
d(f,g) = \sup_{x\in[0,1]} |f(x)-g(x)|.
\]

For $f,g\in C[0,1]$, consider $h(x)=|f(x)-g(x)|$.
$h$ is continuous on the compact set $[0,1]$, so by the Weierstrass theorem it attains its maximum.
Thus
\[
d(f,g)=\max_{x\in[0,1]}|f(x)-g(x)|.
\]

Let $X=\{f\in C[0,1]: f(x)\in[0,1]\ \forall x\in[0,1]\}$.
Consider the sequence $f_n(x)=x^n$. Then $f_n\in X$.

Pointwise, $f_n(x)\to f(x)$ where
\[
f(x)=\begin{cases}
	0 & 0\leq x<1,\\
	1 & x=1.
\end{cases}
\]
This limit function is not continuous. Hence the sequence cannot converge uniformly in $C[0,1]$.

Moreover, any subsequence $(f_{n_k})$ has the same pointwise limit, so no subsequence converges uniformly to a continuous function.
Thus $X$ is not sequentially compact.

Since compactness and sequential compactness are equivalent in metric spaces, $X$ is not compact.

Explicit open cover: For each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, define
\[
U_n=\{f\in X : f(1) > 1 - \tfrac{1}{n}\}.
\]
Also let
\[
V=\{f\in X : f(1)<1\}.
\]
Then $\{V\}\cup\{U_n: n\in\mathbb{N}\}$ is an open cover of $X$.

The constant function $f(x)=1$ belongs only to the sets $U_n$, and to cover it one needs infinitely many such $U_n$. Hence there is no finite subcover.

Therefore $X$ is not compact.

\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0017]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0017}
\par\noindent\textbullet\quad On $C([0,1])$,
	$\|h\|_{L^1}\le \|h\|_{L^2}\le \|h\|_{L^\infty}$ on a set of measure $1$.
	The same argument shows any two of these norm limits must coincide.

The phenomenon of having \emph{two different limits} under two norms does not rely on the supremum
norm itself. It arises whenever we choose two \emph{non-equivalent} and \emph{non-comparable} norms
on the same vector space, so that the identity map between the two normed spaces is not continuous.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{Example beyond supremum norms.}
Consider the vector space $\mathcal{P}$ of real polynomials on $[0,1]$.
Define two different weighted $L^2$ norms:
\[
\|p\|_{2,w_1}=\Big(\int_0^{1/2}|p(t)|^2\,dt\Big)^{1/2},
\qquad
\|p\|_{2,w_2}=\Big(\int_{1/2}^{1}|p(t)|^2\,dt\Big)^{1/2}.
\]
Let $f\in C([0,1])$ satisfy $f|_{[0,1/2]}=0$ and $f|_{[1/2,1]}=1$,
and choose polynomials $p_n\to f$ uniformly (by the Weierstrass theorem).
Then
\[
\|p_n\|_{2,w_1}\to 0,
\qquad
\|p_n-1\|_{2,w_2}\to 0.
\]
Hence the same sequence $(p_n)$ converges to $0$ under $\|\cdot\|_{2,w_1}$
and to $1$ under $\|\cdot\|_{2,w_2}$,
even though neither of these norms is a supremum norm.

\medskip
\noindent\textbf{What is special about the standard norms.}
On standard spaces such as $\ell^p$ or $C([0,1])$ with the usual $L^1,L^2,L^\infty$ norms, we have
\[
\|h\|_{1}\le\|h\|_{2}\le\|h\|_{\infty}.
\]
These norms are \emph{comparable and compatible}, so convergence in any two of them
forces convergence to the \emph{same} limit.
Therefore, different limits can occur only when the norms are \emph{not equivalent}
and not ordered by inequality, not because the supremum norm is involved.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0021]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0021}
\par\noindent\textbullet\quad \textbf{Uniformly continuous:} yes. $f(x)=d(x,\{n^2\})$ is a distance to a closed set,
	hence $|f(x)-f(y)|\le|x-y|$ (1-Lipschitz).
	\textbf{Unbounded:} at $x=\tfrac{n^2+(n+1)^2}{2}$ we have $f(x)=\tfrac{2n+1}{2}\to\infty$.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0022]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0022}
Closed sets in $C([0,1])$ under $\|\cdot\|_\infty$ vs.\ $\|\cdot\|_1$

Is the set $\{f: f(1/2)=0\}$ closed in the space $\big(C([0,1]),\|\cdot\|_\infty\big)$?
What about the set $\{f:\int_0^1 f=0\}$?
In each case, does the answer change if we replace the uniform norm with the norm $\|\cdot\|_1$?

In a normed space, the zero set of a continuous linear functional is closed.
For $C([0,1])$,
the evaluation functional $E_{x_0}(f)=f(x_0)$ is continuous under $\|\cdot\|_\infty$ (since $|f(x_0)|\le\|f\|_\infty$),
but not under $\|\cdot\|_1$.
The integral functional $I(f)=\int_0^1 f$ is continuous under both norms, because
$|I(f)|\le\int_0^1 |f|\le \|f\|_\infty$ and also $|I(f)|\le \|f\|_1$.

	\par\noindent \textbf{A.}\quad $A=\{f\in C([0,1]): f(1/2)=0\}$.

		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Under $\|\cdot\|_\infty$: $A=E_{1/2}^{-1}(\{0\})$ with $E_{1/2}$ continuous, hence $A$ is closed.
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Under $\|\cdot\|_1$: $A$ is not closed. Define $g\equiv 1$. For $n\ge 2$ let $f_n$ equal $1$ outside $[1/2-1/n,\,1/2+1/n]$, and on this interval let $f_n$ be the linear tent with $f_n(1/2)=0$ and $f_n(1/2\pm 1/n)=1$. Then $f_n\in A$ and
		$\|f_n-g\|_1$ equals the area of the tent, $\frac12\cdot \frac{2}{n}\cdot 1=\frac1n\to 0$, so $f_n\to g$ in $\|\cdot\|_1$ while $g\notin A$.

	\par\noindent \textbf{B.}\quad $B=\{f\in C([0,1]): \int_0^1 f=0\}$.

		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Under $\|\cdot\|_\infty$: $B=I^{-1}(\{0\})$ with $I$ continuous since $|\int_0^1 f|\le \|f\|_\infty$, hence $B$ is closed.
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Under $\|\cdot\|_1$: likewise $|\int_0^1 f|\le \|f\|_1$, so $I$ is continuous and $B$ is closed.

\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
	\text{Set} & (C([0,1]),\|\cdot\|_\infty) & (C([0,1]),\|\cdot\|_1)\\ \hline
	\{f: f(1/2)=0\} & \text{closed} & \text{not closed}\\
	\{f: \int_0^1 f=0\} & \text{closed} & \text{closed}
\end{array}
\]
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0028]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0028}
\par\noindent\textbullet\quad \textbf{Not complete in $\|\cdot\|_{1}$.}
	Let $h=\mathbf{1}_{[0,1/2]}$ (discontinuous). Choose $h_k\in C([0,1])$ that converge to $h$ in $L^{1}$,
	e.g.\ smooth ramps replacing the jump over width $1/k$. Then $\|h_k-h\|_{1}\to 0$, so $(h_k)$ is Cauchy in
	$\|\cdot\|_{1}$ but its limit is not continuous. Thus $\big(C([0,1]),\|\cdot\|_{1}\big)$ is not complete.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0029]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0029}
Suppose $f \in L^p_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is a periodic function.
Fix $\varepsilon>0$, and define
\[
Q = \{x\in\mathbb{R}^n : |x_j| < 1,\ j=1,\dots,n\},
\qquad
q = \{x\in\mathbb{R}^n : |x_j| < \tfrac12,\ j=1,\dots,n\}.
\]

Show that there exists $h_\varepsilon \in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$ with
$\operatorname{supp} h_\varepsilon \subset Q$ such that
\[
\|f\,\mathbf{1}_q - h_\varepsilon\|_{L^p(\mathbb{R}^n)} < \varepsilon.
\]
Define
\[
f_\varepsilon = \sum_{g\in\mathbb{Z}^n} \tau_g h_\varepsilon.
\]

Show that $f_\varepsilon$ is smooth and periodic.

Show that there exists a constant $c_n$, depending only on $n$, such that
\[
\|f - f_\varepsilon\|_{L^p(q)} < c_n\,\varepsilon.
\]

Let $f\in L^p_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ be a periodic function with
period lattice $\mathbb{Z}^n$.  It is natural to think of $f$ as a
function on the torus $\mathbb{T}^n=\mathbb{R}^n/\mathbb{Z}^n$.  The
goal of Exercise~5.4 is to approximate $f$ in $L^p$ on a fundamental
domain by smooth periodic functions.

We work with the cubes
\[
Q = \{x\in\mathbb{R}^n : |x_j|<1,\ j=1,\dots,n\},
\qquad
q = \{x\in\mathbb{R}^n : |x_j|<\tfrac12,\ j=1,\dots,n\}.
\]
A standard fact in $L^p$--theory is that $C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is
dense in $L^p(\mathbb{R}^n)$ for $1\le p<\infty$.  In particular, any
$L^p$-function supported in a bounded set can be approximated in $L^p$
by smooth compactly supported functions with support in a slightly
larger set.  Applying this to $f\mathbf{1}_q$ gives, for each
$\varepsilon>0$, the existence of $h_\varepsilon\in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$
with $\operatorname{supp}h_\varepsilon\subset Q$ and
$\|f\mathbf{1}_q-h_\varepsilon\|_{L^p(\mathbb{R}^n)}<\varepsilon$.

The function
\[
f_\varepsilon(x) := \sum_{g\in\mathbb{Z}^n} h_\varepsilon(x-g)
\]
is then well-defined, because at each $x$ only finitely many translates
$x-g$ lie in $Q$ and contribute to the sum.  Being locally a finite sum
of smooth functions, $f_\varepsilon$ is smooth.  Moreover,
$f_\varepsilon$ is $\mathbb{Z}^n$--periodic, since for any $k\in\mathbb{Z}^n$
\[
f_\varepsilon(x+k)
= \sum_{g\in\mathbb{Z}^n} h_\varepsilon(x+k-g)
= \sum_{h\in\mathbb{Z}^n} h_\varepsilon(x-h)
= f_\varepsilon(x),
\]
after the change of variables $h=g-k$.

To control $\|f-f_\varepsilon\|_{L^p(q)}$ one uses the periodicity of $f$
and the fact that $h_\varepsilon$ approximates $f\mathbf{1}_q$ on each
translate $q+g$ in the same way.  Since at most a bounded number of
translates of $Q$ overlap a given point (a constant depending only on
$n$), the total error on $q$ is controlled by a finite multiple of the
error $\|f\mathbf{1}_q-h_\varepsilon\|_{L^p}$.  This yields a bound
$\|f - f_\varepsilon\|_{L^p(q)} \le c_n\varepsilon$, where $c_n>0$
depends only on the dimension.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0030]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0030}
— Complete metric on $(0,1]$ with the usual topology

Find a metric \(d\) on \(X=(0,1]\) such that \((X,d)\) is complete and such that the \(d\)-open subsets
are exactly the open subsets of \(X\) in the Euclidean subspace topology.

\smallskip

If \(\phi:(X,\tau)\to(Y,\rho)\) is a homeomorphism and \((Y,\rho)\) is complete, then
\(d(x,y):=\rho(\phi(x),\phi(y))\) makes \((X,d)\) complete and induces \(\tau\).

For \(X=(0,1]\), the map \(\phi(x)=1/x\) is a homeomorphism onto \([1,\infty)\),
which is complete under the Euclidean metric.

\smallskip
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Define
\[
d(x,y)=\Bigl|\frac{1}{x}-\frac{1}{y}\Bigr|,\qquad x,y\in(0,1].
\]

\noindent\textbf{Same topology.}
The metric \(d\) is the pullback of the Euclidean metric on \([1,\infty)\) via the homeomorphism \(\phi(x)=1/x\).
Hence the \(d\)-open sets coincide with the Euclidean-open sets of \((0,1]\).

\smallskip

\noindent\textbf{Completeness.}
Let \((x_n)\) be \(d\)-Cauchy. Then \((\phi(x_n))=(1/x_n)\) is Cauchy in \([1,\infty)\),
so \(\phi(x_n)\to L\in[1,\infty)\).
Therefore \(x_n=\phi^{-1}(\phi(x_n))\to \phi^{-1}(L)=1/L\in(0,1]\).
Thus every \(d\)-Cauchy sequence converges in \(X\), i.e., \((X,d)\) is complete.

\smallskip
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0035]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0035}
In higher dimensions, let $f(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{1+x^2+y^2}$ on the closed disk
		$E=\{(x,y):x^2+y^2\leq 10\}$.
		The domain is compact and $f$ is continuous, so $f$ is uniformly continuous.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0052]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0052}
(exact).

Let $f:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}$ be bounded. Recall that Lebesgue's theorem says that $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$ if and only if the set $D_f$ of points in $[a,b]$ where $f$ is discontinuous has Lebesgue measure zero. (By definition, a set $D\subset\mathbb{R}$ has Lebesgue measure zero if for every $\varepsilon>0$, there is a countable collection of open intervals $I_j=(a_j,b_j)$ such that $D\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty I_j$ and $\sum_{j=1}^\infty |I_j|<\varepsilon$, where $|I_j|=b_j-a_j$.) As an optional exercise, prove this theorem by completing the outline below. We shall use the notation as in lectures, so $U(P,f),L(P,f)$ denote the upper and lower sums for $f$ relative to a partition $P$ of $[a,b]$.

	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Show that $y\in D_f\cap(a,b)$ (i.e.\ $y$ is an interior discontinuity) if and only if there exists $\varepsilon=\varepsilon_y>0$ such that $\sup_I f-\inf_I f>\varepsilon$ for every open interval $I\subset (a,b)$ with $y\in I$. Hence $D_f\cap(a,b)=\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty E_j$, where $E_j=\{y\in(a,b):\ \sup_I f-\inf_I f>j^{-1}\ \text{for every open interval }I\text{ with }y\in I\}$.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Suppose that $f$ is Riemann integrable. It suffices to show that $E_j$ has Lebesgue measure zero for each $j$ (Why?). Fix $j$, let $\varepsilon>0$ and choose a partition $P=\{a=a_0<a_1<\cdots<a_n=b\}$ such that $U(P,f)-L(P,f)<j^{-1}\varepsilon$. Let $K=\{k:\ E_j\cap(a_k,a_{k+1})\neq\emptyset\}$. Then $E_j\setminus\{a_0,a_1,\dots,a_n\}\subset\bigcup_{k\in K}(a_k,a_{k+1})$. Show that $\sum_{k\in K}(a_{k+1}-a_k)<\varepsilon$. Deduce that $E_j$ has Lebesgue measure zero.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Now suppose that $D_f$ has Lebesgue measure zero. Let $\varepsilon>0$, and choose open intervals $I_j\subset\mathbb{R}$, $j=1,2,\dots$, with $D_f\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty I_j$ and $\sum_{j=1}^\infty |I_j|<\varepsilon$. Let $F=[a,b]\setminus\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty I_j$. Show that there exists $\delta>0$ such that the following holds: $x\in F,\ y\in F,\ |x-y|<\delta \Rightarrow |f(x)-f(y)|<\varepsilon$. [This is a strengthening of the theorem we proved in lecture that says that a continuous function on a closed, bounded interval (or more generally on a compact metric space) is uniformly continuous, but the same contradiction argument we used in fact works here.] Let $P=\{a=a_0<a_1<\cdots<a_n=b\}$ be any partition of $[a,b]$ such that $a_{j+1}-a_j<\delta$, and let $J=\{j:\ [a_j,a_{j+1}]\cap F\neq\emptyset\}$. Show that $\sup_{[a_j,a_{j+1}]} f-\inf_{[a_j,a_{j+1}]} f<2\varepsilon$ for each $j\in J$, and that $\bigcup_{j\notin J}(a_j,a_{j+1})\subset\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty I_j$. Conclude that $U(P,f)-L(P,f)<2(b-a+\sup_{[a,b]}|f|)\varepsilon$, and hence that $f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$.

 \mbox{}\par

For a partition $P=\{a=a_0<\cdots<a_n=b\}$ and bounded $f$,
\begin{center}
\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
U(P,f)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1} M_k\,(a_{k+1}-a_k),\qquad
M_k=\sup_{[a_k,a_{k+1}]} f,\qquad
L(P,f)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1} m_k\,(a_{k+1}-a_k),\qquad
m_k=\inf_{[a_k,a_{k+1}]} f.
$}
\end{center}
Let the \emph{oscillation} on an interval $I$ be $\omega(f;I)=\sup_I f-\inf_I f$.
Then
\[
U(P,f)-L(P,f)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1} \omega\!\big(f;[a_k,a_{k+1}]\big)\,(a_{k+1}-a_k).
\]
A bounded $f$ is Riemann integrable iff $\forall \eta>0$ there exists $P$ with $U(P,f)-L(P,f)<\eta$.

 \mbox{}\par

The \emph{oscillation at a point} $x$ is
\[
\omega(f;x)=\inf_{\delta>0}\ \sup\{|f(u)-f(v)|:\ u,v\in (x-\delta,x+\delta)\cap[a,b]\}.
\]
Then $f$ is continuous at $x$ iff $\omega(f;x)=0$.
Equivalently: $x\in D_f$ iff $\exists\,\varepsilon>0$ such that $\omega(f;I)>\varepsilon$
for every interval $I\ni x$.
Define
\[
E_j=\Big\{x\in(a,b):\ \omega(f;I)>\tfrac{1}{j}\ \text{for every open }I\ni x\Big\}.
\]
Then $D_f\cap(a,b)=\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty E_j$.

 \mbox{}\par

A set $N\subset\mathbb{R}$ has Lebesgue measure zero iff
for every $\varepsilon>0$ there exist open intervals $I_j$ with
$N\subset \bigcup_{j=1}^\infty I_j$ and $\sum_{j=1}^\infty |I_j|<\varepsilon$.
If $E\subset \bigcup_{k\in K}(a_k,a_{k+1})$, then
\[
\lambda(E)\le \sum_{k\in K}(a_{k+1}-a_k).
\]

 \mbox{}\par
If $F\subset [a,b]$ is closed, then $F$ is compact.
If $f$ is continuous at each point of $F$, then $f|_F$ is continuous $F\to\mathbb{R}$,
hence (Heine--Cantor) uniformly continuous on $F$:
\[
\forall \varepsilon>0\ \exists\,\delta>0\ \forall x,y\in F\quad
|x-y|<\delta \ \Longrightarrow\ |f(x)-f(y)|<\varepsilon.
\]

  \mbox{}\par
For $y\in(a,b)$ define the oscillation
\[
\omega(f;y):=\inf_{\delta>0}\Big(\sup_{(y-\delta,y+\delta)\cap[a,b]} f
-\inf_{(y-\delta,y+\delta)\cap[a,b]} f\Big).
\]
It is standard that $f$ is continuous at $y$ iff $\omega(f;y)=0$.

\emph{($\Rightarrow$)} Suppose $y\in D_f\cap(a,b)$. If no $\varepsilon_y>0$ satisfied
$\sup_I f-\inf_I f>\varepsilon_y$ for every open interval $I\ni y$, then for each $m\in\mathbb N$
we could choose an interval $I_m\ni y$ with $\sup_{I_m} f-\inf_{I_m} f\le 1/m$. Taking $I_m=(y-\delta_m,y+\delta_m)$ with $\delta_m\downarrow 0$ gives $\omega(f;y)\le 1/m$ for all $m$, hence $\omega(f;y)=0$, a contradiction. Thus there exists $\varepsilon_y>0$ such that
$\sup_I f-\inf_I f>\varepsilon_y$ for every open interval $I\ni y$.

\emph{($\Leftarrow$)} Conversely, if there exists $\varepsilon_y>0$ with
$\sup_I f-\inf_I f>\varepsilon_y$ for every open interval $I\ni y$, then for all $\delta>0$,
\[
\sup_{(y-\delta,y+\delta)} f-\inf_{(y-\delta,y+\delta)} f\ge \varepsilon_y,
\]
so $\omega(f;y)\ge \varepsilon_y>0$ and hence $f$ is discontinuous at $y$.

Consequently,
\[
D_f\cap(a,b)=\bigcup_{j=1}^\infty E_j,
\qquad
E_j:=\Big\{y\in(a,b):\ \sup_I f-\inf_I f>\tfrac1j \ \text{for every open interval } I\ni y\Big\}.
\]
Indeed, $y\in D_f\cap(a,b)$ iff $\omega(f;y)>0$, i.e.\ iff $1/j<\omega(f;y)$ for some $j$,
which is equivalent to $y\in E_j$ for some $j$.

  \mbox{}\par
Fix $j\in\mathbb N$ and $\varepsilon>0$. Take a partition
$P=\{a=a_0<\cdots<a_n=b\}$ with $U(P,f)-L(P,f)<\varepsilon/j$.
Let $K=\{k:\ E_j\cap(a_k,a_{k+1})\neq\emptyset\}$.
For $k\in K$, pick $y_k\in E_j\cap(a_k,a_{k+1})$; then with $I=(a_k,a_{k+1})$,
\[
\sup_I f-\inf_I f>\tfrac1j,
\]
hence $M_k-m_k\ge\tfrac1j$. Therefore
\[
\sum_{k\in K}(a_{k+1}-a_k)
\le j\sum_{k\in K}(M_k-m_k)(a_{k+1}-a_k)
\le j\big(U(P,f)-L(P,f)\big)<\varepsilon.
\]
Thus $E_j\setminus\{a_0,\dots,a_n\}$ is covered by $\bigcup_{k\in K}(a_k,a_{k+1})$ of
total length $<\varepsilon$, so $E_j$ has measure zero.

 \mbox{}\par
Let $\varepsilon>0$ and choose open intervals $I_\ell$ with
$D_f\subset\bigcup_\ell I_\ell$ and $\sum_\ell |I_\ell|<\varepsilon$.
Put $F=[a,b]\setminus\bigcup_\ell I_\ell$.
Then $F$ is compact and $f|_F$ is uniformly continuous:
$\exists\delta>0$ such that $x,y\in F$, $|x-y|<\delta\Rightarrow |f(x)-f(y)|<\varepsilon$.

Refine a partition $P=\{a=a_0<\cdots<a_n=b\}$ so that
(i) $a_{k+1}-a_k<\delta$ for all $k$, and
(ii) every endpoint of every $I_\ell$ is a partition point.
Let $J=\{k:\ [a_k,a_{k+1}]\subset F\}$.
For $k\in J$,
\[
\sup_{[a_k,a_{k+1}]} f - \inf_{[a_k,a_{k+1}]} f < \varepsilon.
\]
For $k\notin J$ we have $[a_k,a_{k+1}]\subset I_\ell$ for some $\ell$, hence
$\sum_{k\notin J}(a_{k+1}-a_k)\le \sum_\ell |I_\ell|<\varepsilon$.
Let $M=\sup_{[a,b]}|f|$. Then
\[
\begin{aligned}
	U(P,f)-L(P,f)
	&=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\big(\sup_{[a_k,a_{k+1}]} f-\inf_{[a_k,a_{k+1}]} f\big)\,(a_{k+1}-a_k)\\
	&< \varepsilon\sum_{k\in J}(a_{k+1}-a_k)+2M\sum_{k\notin J}(a_{k+1}-a_k)\\
	&\le \varepsilon(b-a)+2M\,\varepsilon.
\end{aligned}
\]
Since $\varepsilon>0$ is arbitrary, $U(P,f)-L(P,f)$ can be made arbitrarily small; hence
$f$ is Riemann integrable on $[a,b]$.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0062]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0062}
\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Compactly supported mollifiers $\phi_\varepsilon$ live in
	$\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and are suitable for regularizing
	arbitrary distributions in $\mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^n)$ (at least
	away from the boundary in a domain).
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0069]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0069}
(i) Let $x$ be a point in the closed unit disc $D=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^2 : \|z\|\leq 1\}$, let $K\subseteq D$ be compact not containing $x$, and define $f_x:K\to \partial D$ as follows: for $y\in K$, take the line segment from $x$ to $y$ and extend it until it first hits the boundary $\partial D$. Call this point $f_x(y)$. Prove that $f_x$ is continuous.

(ii) Show that if $K$ is connected, then $f_x(K)$ is connected in $\partial D$.

---
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\section*{Solution (i)}
For $y\in K$, consider the ray
\[
\ell(t) = x + t(y-x), \quad t\geq 0.
\]
We want $\|\ell(t)\|=1$, i.e.
\[
\|x+t(y-x)\|^2=1.
\]
Solving for $t$ gives
\[
\lambda(y) = \frac{-\langle x, y-x \rangle + \sqrt{\langle x,y-x\rangle^2 + \|y-x\|^2(1-\|x\|^2)}}{\|y-x\|^2}.
\]
Thus
\[
f_x(y) = x + \lambda(y)(y-x).
\]
The denominator $\|y-x\|^2>0$ since $y\neq x$, and the square root is continuous. Therefore $\lambda(y)$ is continuous, hence $f_x$ is continuous.

---
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0070]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0070}
— Dual of a normed space is Banach; dual of the completion

Let $X$ be a normed space and $X'$ its dual with the operator norm.

		\par\noindent (a)\quad Show that $X'$ is complete (a Banach space).
		\par\noindent (b)\quad If $\overline{X}$ is the completion of $X$ (same norm), show that the restriction map
		$J:\overline{X}'\to X'$, $J(\Phi)=\Phi|_X$, is an isometric isomorphism. In particular $X'\cong \overline{X}'$.

	If $(\Lambda_n)$ is Cauchy in operator norm, then for each $x\in X$ the scalar sequence $(\Lambda_n x)$ is Cauchy.
	Hahn--Banach guarantees an isometric extension of bounded linear functionals from a dense subspace; when the subspace is dense, the extension is unique.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
(a) Let $(\Lambda_n)$ be Cauchy in $X'$. For each $x\in X$ the sequence $(\Lambda_n x)$ is Cauchy in $\mathbb K$, hence convergent. Define $\Lambda x:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\Lambda_n x$. Linearity of $\Lambda$ follows from pointwise limits of linear maps. For $\|x\|\le 1$,
	\[
	|\Lambda x|=\lim_{n}|\Lambda_n x|\le \liminf_{n}\|\Lambda_n\|,
	\]
	so $\Lambda$ is bounded with $\|\Lambda\|\le \liminf_n \|\Lambda_n\|$. Moreover,
	\[
	\|\Lambda_n-\Lambda\|
	=\sup_{\|x\|\le 1} |\Lambda_n x-\Lambda x|
	=\sup_{\|x\|\le 1}\lim_{m} |\Lambda_n x-\Lambda_m x|
	\le \limsup_{m}\|\Lambda_n-\Lambda_m\|\xrightarrow[n]{}0.
	\]
	Hence $X'$ is complete.

	\smallskip
	(b) For $\Phi\in\overline{X}'$, $\|J(\Phi)\|\le \|\Phi\|$ is immediate. Conversely, for $z\in\overline{X}$ take $x_k\in X$ with $x_k\to z$; then
	$|\Phi z|=\lim_k |\Phi x_k| \le \|J(\Phi)\|\|z\|$, so $\|\Phi\|\le \|J(\Phi)\|$. Thus $J$ is an isometry.
	Given $\Lambda\in X'$, Hahn--Banach yields an extension $\widetilde\Lambda\in\overline{X}'$ with $\|\widetilde\Lambda\|=\|\Lambda\|$; density of $X$ in $\overline{X}$ makes the extension unique. Therefore $J$ is bijective and $X'\cong \overline{X}'$ isometrically.

	\bigskip
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0072]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0072}
\par\noindent\textbullet\quad $f\in L^\infty$ with compact support (e.g.\ $(1+x^2)^{-1}\mathbf 1_{[-5,5]}$):
	the same argument applies since $f\in L^1$.

\bigskip\hrule\bigskip
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0073]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0073}
(i)

Let $x$ be a point in the closed unit disc $D=\{z\in\mathbb{R}^2 : \|z\|\leq 1\}$, let $K\subseteq D$ be compact not containing $x$, and define $f_x:K\to \partial D$ as follows: for $y\in K$, take the line segment from $x$ to $y$ and extend it until it first hits the boundary $\partial D$. Call this point $f_x(y)$. Prove that $f_x$ is continuous.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
\textbf{Step 1. Explicit formula.}
For $y\in K$, consider the ray
\[
\ell(t) = x + t(y-x), \quad t\geq 0.
\]
We seek $t=\lambda(y)$ such that $\|\ell(t)\|=1$, i.e.
\[
\|x + t(y-x)\|^2 = 1.
\]
Solving this quadratic yields
\[
\lambda(y) = \frac{-\langle x, y-x \rangle + \sqrt{\langle x,y-x\rangle^2 + \|y-x\|^2(1-\|x\|^2)}}{\|y-x\|^2}.
\]
Thus
\[
f_x(y) = x + \lambda(y)(y-x).
\]

\medskip

\textbf{Step 2. Continuity of $\lambda(y)$.}
The expression for $\lambda(y)$ is continuous in $y$, since the denominator $\|y-x\|^2>0$ (because $y\neq x$) and the square root involves only continuous operations. Therefore, $\lambda(y)$ is continuous.

\medskip

\textbf{Step 3. Continuity of $f_x(y)$.}
Since $f_x(y) = x + \lambda(y)(y-x)$, and both $y\mapsto (y-x)$ and $y\mapsto \lambda(y)$ are continuous, the composition is continuous. Hence $f_x$ is continuous.

\medskip
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0075]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0075}
Equivalent Norms and Their Topologies

Let $\|\cdot\|_1$ and $\|\cdot\|_2$ be two norms on a vector space $X$.
Show that the following are equivalent:

	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad The norms are equivalent: $\exists a,b>0$ such that $a\|x\|_1\le\|x\|_2\le b\|x\|_1$ for all $x\in X$;
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad The topologies induced by $\|\cdot\|_1$ and $\|\cdot\|_2$ are the same;
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad The convergent sequences are the same for both norms.

Each norm induces a metric $d_i(x,y)=\|x-y\|_i$ and hence a topology.
Two norms are called \emph{equivalent} if there exist constants $a,b>0$
with $a\|x\|_1\le\|x\|_2\le b\|x\|_1$ for all $x$.
Then both norms yield the same open sets, convergence, and continuity.
Conversely, identical convergence or topology implies the existence of such $a,b$.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}

We prove the cycle
\[
(1)\Rightarrow(2)\Rightarrow(3)\Rightarrow(1).
\]

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{(1)$\Rightarrow$(2).}\quad
Assume $a\|x\|_1 \le \|x\|_2 \le b\|x\|_1$.
Let $B_i(x_0,r)$ denote the open ball in $(X,\|\cdot\|_i)$.

	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad If $y\in B_1(x_0,r)$, then $\|y-x_0\|_1 < r$.
	Hence $\|y-x_0\|_2 < br$, so $y\in B_2(x_0,br)$.
	Thus $B_1(x_0,r)\subseteq B_2(x_0,br)$.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Conversely, from $a\|x\|_1\le\|x\|_2$, we get
	$B_2(x_0,r)\subseteq B_1(x_0,r/a)$.

So open balls in one norm contain open balls of the other.
Hence both topologies have the same open sets.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{(2)$\Rightarrow$(3).}\quad
If the two topologies coincide, then convergence (defined by open neighborhoods)
is identical. Thus a sequence converges in one norm if and only if it converges in the other.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{(3)$\Rightarrow$(1).}\quad
Assume the same convergent sequences.
We show existence of $a,b>0$.

Define $f(x)=\|x\|_2$.
If no $b$ exists with $\|x\|_2\le b\|x\|_1$,
we could find a sequence $x_n$ with $\|x_n\|_1=1$ and $\|x_n\|_2>n$.
Then $x_n/n\to 0$ in $\|\cdot\|_1$ but not in $\|\cdot\|_2$, contradicting (3).
Thus $\exists b>0$ s.t.\ $\|x\|_2\le b\|x\|_1$.

Similarly, interchanging norms gives $a>0$ s.t.\ $a\|x\|_1\le\|x\|_2$.
Hence the norms are equivalent.
\end{solution}

Let $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^n\times\mathbb R^m)$ and, for $y\in\mathbb R^m$, define $\phi_y(x)=\phi(x,y)$.
Let $u\in\mathcal D'(\mathbb R^n)$.

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0085]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0085}
— Expanding maps on a compact space are onto

Let \((X,d)\) be a compact metric space and \(f:X\to X\) be continuous with
\[
d(f(x),f(y)) \ge d(x,y)\qquad (\forall x,y\in X).
\]
Prove that \(f\) is surjective.

\smallskip

\noindent\textbf{Compact images are compact.}
If \(X\) is compact and \(f:X\to X\) is continuous, then \(f(X)\) is compact (hence closed) in \(X\).

\smallskip

\noindent\textbf{Positive distance from a compact set.}
If \(C\subset X\) is compact and \(x\notin C\), then
\(\operatorname{dist}(x,C)=\min_{z\in C} d(x,z)>0\).

\smallskip

\noindent\textbf{Sequential compactness.}
In metric spaces, compactness \(\Leftrightarrow\) every sequence admits a convergent subsequence.

\smallskip

\noindent\textbf{Monotone gaps under expansion.}
From \(d(fu,fv)\ge d(u,v)\) it follows inductively that for the orbit \(x_{n+1}=f(x_n)\),
the differences satisfy \(d(x_n,x_{n+1})\ge d(x_{n-1},x_n)\) for all \(n\).

\smallskip

\noindent\textbf{Injectivity (useful side fact).}
If \(d(fx,fy)\ge d(x,y)\) and \(f(x)=f(y)\), then \(0\ge d(x,y)\Rightarrow x=y\); hence \(f\) is injective.

\smallskip
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
Assume \(f\) is not surjective. Choose \(x_0\in X\setminus f(X)\).

Since \(f(X)\) is compact, the distance
\[
r=\operatorname{dist}(x_0,f(X))=\min_{y\in f(X)} d(x_0,y)
\]
satisfies \(r>0\). Hence \(d(x_0,y)\ge r\) for all \(y\in f(X)\).

\smallskip

Consider the sequence \(x_{n+1}=f(x_n)\). Then \(x_n\in f(X)\) for all \(n\ge1\), so
\(d(x_0,x_1)\ge r\).
Using \(d(fu,fv)\ge d(u,v)\) iteratively, we obtain
\[
d(x_n,x_{n+1}) \ \ge\ d(x_{n-1},x_n) \ \ge \ \cdots \ \ge\ d(x_0,x_1)\ \ge\ r
\qquad (n\ge1).
\]
Therefore \((x_n)\) is not Cauchy and hence has no convergent subsequence.

\smallskip

This contradicts the sequential compactness of \(X\). Therefore \(f\) must be surjective.

\smallskip
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0086]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0086}
Distributions and their orders

\textbf{(a)} Show that $\delta_x$, as defined in Example~7, is continuous and
linear, hence a distribution. Find its order.

\textbf{(b)} Show that $T_f$, as defined in Example~7, is continuous and
linear, hence a distribution. Find its order.

\textbf{(c)} By constructing a suitable sequence of smooth functions show
that the order of $\mathrm{P.V.}\!\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)$ is one.

\bigskip

	For fixed $x\in\mathbb{R}^n$ the functional $\delta_x$ is defined by
	\[
	\delta_x(\varphi) := \varphi(x), \qquad \varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^n).
	\]
	It is clearly linear:
	\[
	\delta_x(\alpha\varphi+\beta\psi)
	= (\alpha\varphi+\beta\psi)(x)
	= \alpha\varphi(x)+\beta\psi(x)
	= \alpha\,\delta_x(\varphi)+\beta\,\delta_x(\psi).
	\]

	To show continuity and determine its order, let $K\subset\mathbb{R}^n$
	be compact and suppose $\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset K$.
	If $x\notin K$ then $\delta_x(\varphi)=0$.  If $x\in K$, then
	\[
	|\delta_x(\varphi)| = |\varphi(x)| \le \sup_{y\in K}|\varphi(y)|.
	\]
	Thus for each compact $K$ we can choose $C_K=1$ and obtain
	\[
	|\delta_x(\varphi)|
	\le C_K \sup_{K}|\varphi|.
	\]
	Hence $\delta_x$ is a distribution of order $0$.

	Let $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and define
	\[
	T_f(\varphi) := \int_{\mathbb{R}^n} f(x)\,\varphi(x)\,dx.
	\]
	Linearity is immediate from linearity of the integral.  For continuity,
	fix a compact set $K\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ and suppose
	$\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset K$. Then
	\[
	|T_f(\varphi)|
	= \left|\int_{K} f(x)\varphi(x)\,dx\right|
	\le \int_{K} |f(x)|\,|\varphi(x)|\,dx
	\le \Bigl(\int_{K} |f(x)|\,dx\Bigr) \sup_{K}|\varphi(x)|.
	\]
	Setting $C_K := \int_{K}|f(x)|\,dx<\infty$, we obtain
	\[
	|T_f(\varphi)| \le C_K \sup_{K}|\varphi|,
	\]
	so $T_f$ is a distribution of order $0$.

	\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{(c) The order of $\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$.}\quad
	The principal value distribution $\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$ acts on
	$\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})$ by
	\[
	\bigl\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi \bigr\rangle
	:= \lim_{\varepsilon\to0^+}
	\int_{|x|>\varepsilon} \frac{\varphi(x)}{x}\,dx.
	\]

	\emph{Step 1: $\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$ has order at most $1$.}
	One shows (by integration by parts on the truncated integrals and
	passing to the limit) that
	\[
	\bigl\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi \bigr\rangle
	= -\int_{\mathbb{R}} \log|x|\,\varphi'(x)\,dx.
	\]
	Let $K\subset\mathbb{R}$ be compact and $\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset K$.
	Then
	\[
	\left|\bigl\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi \bigr\rangle\right|
	\le \int_{K}|\log|x||\,|\varphi'(x)|\,dx
	\le \Bigl(\int_{K}|\log|x||\,dx\Bigr)\sup_{K}|\varphi'(x)|.
	\]
	Thus there is a constant $C_K$ such that
	\[
	\left|\bigl\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi \bigr\rangle\right|
	\le C_K \sup_{K}|\varphi'|,
	\]
	which means that $\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$ is a distribution of order
	$\le 1$.

	\emph{Step 2: $\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$ is not of order $0$.}
	Suppose, towards a contradiction, that it had order $0$.  Then for
	$K=[-1,1]$ there would exist $C>0$ such that for all
	$\varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})$ with
	$\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset[-1,1]$,
	\[
	\left|\bigl\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi \bigr\rangle\right|
	\le C\sup_{[-1,1]}|\varphi|.
	\tag{$*$}
	\]

	Choose $\eta\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R})$ with $0\le\eta\le1$,
	$\eta(x)=0$ for $x\le0$ and $\eta(x)=1$ for $x\ge1$, and let
	$\chi\in C_c^\infty((-1,1))$ satisfy $\chi\equiv1$ on $[-1/2,1/2]$.
	Define
	\[
	\varphi_n(x) := \eta(nx)\,\chi(x).
	\]
	Then $\varphi_n\in C_c^\infty((-1,1))$, $0\le\varphi_n\le1$ and
	$\sup|\varphi_n|=1$ for all $n$.  Moreover, for $x\in[1/n,\,1/2]$ we
	have $\varphi_n(x)=1$.

	For these test functions the principal value reduces to an improper
	integral:
	\[
	\bigl\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi_n \bigr\rangle
	= \int_{0}^{1} \frac{\varphi_n(x)}{x}\,dx.
	\]
	Hence
	\[
	\bigl\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi_n \bigr\rangle
	\ge \int_{1/n}^{1/2} \frac{1}{x}\,dx - \int_{0}^{1/n}\frac{|\varphi_n(x)|}{x}\,dx
	= \log n + \log\!\left(\tfrac12\right) - R_n,
	\]
	where $R_n$ is a bounded remainder.  Consequently
	\[
	\bigl|\bigl\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi_n \bigr\rangle\bigr|
	\to \infty \quad\text{as } n\to\infty,
	\]
	while $\sup_{[-1,1]}|\varphi_n|=1$.  This contradicts $(*)$, so
	$\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$ cannot be of order $0$.

	Combining the two steps, we conclude that the order of
	$\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$ is exactly $1$.

\begin{definition}[Regular distributions]
	Let $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^n)$.
	The functional
	\[
	T_f(\varphi)
	:= \int_{\mathbb{R}^n} f(x)\,\varphi(x)\,dx,
	\qquad \varphi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^n),
	\]
	is called the \emph{regular distribution} associated with $f$.
	Every regular distribution has order~$0$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Order of a distribution]
	A distribution $T\in\mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^n)$ has \emph{order $\le m$}
	if for every compact $K\subset\mathbb{R}^n$ there exists $C_K>0$ such that
	\[
	|\langle T, \varphi\rangle|
	\le C_K \sum_{|\alpha|\le m} \sup_{x\in K}
	\left| \partial^\alpha \varphi(x) \right|,
	\qquad \operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset K.
	\]
	The smallest such $m$ is called the \emph{order} of $T$.
\end{definition}

\medskip

\[
\delta_x(\varphi) := \varphi(x).
\]
For compact $K\ni x$,
\[
|\delta_x(\varphi)| = |\varphi(x)|
\le \sup_K |\varphi|.
\]
Thus $\delta_x$ is linear, continuous, and of \textbf{order $0$}.

\medskip

If $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^n)$,
\[
T_f(\varphi)=\int f(x)\varphi(x)\,dx.
\]
If $\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset K$,
\[
|T_f(\varphi)|
\le \left(\int_K |f|\right)
\sup_K |\varphi|.
\]
Thus $T_f$ is of \textbf{order $0$}.

\medskip

Let $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ and define
\[
T_f(\varphi) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^n} f(x)\,\varphi(x)\,dx.
\]
If $\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset K$, then
\[
|T_f(\varphi)|
\le \int_{K} |f(x)|\,|\varphi(x)|\,dx
\le \Bigl(\int_{K} |f(x)|\,dx\Bigr)\sup_{K}|\varphi(x)|.
\]
Hence $T_f$ is linear, continuous, and of order $0$.

\par\medskip\noindent\textbf{3. Principal value distribution $\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$ (order $1$).}\quad
\[
\left\langle \mathrm{P.V.}\!\left(\frac{1}{x}\right), \varphi \right\rangle
:= \lim_{\varepsilon\to0^+}
\int_{|x|>\varepsilon} \frac{\varphi(x)}{x}\,dx.
\]
One shows that
\[
\left\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi\right\rangle
= -\int_{\mathbb{R}}\log|x|\,\varphi'(x)\,dx.
\]
Thus
\[
|\langle \mathrm{P.V.}(1/x), \varphi\rangle|
\le C_K \sup_K |\varphi'|.
\]
So $\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$ has \textbf{order $\le1$}.
It is not of order $0$, hence the order is \textbf{exactly $1$}.

\medskip

By definition,
\[
\langle \delta_0', \varphi\rangle := -\varphi'(0).
\]
Therefore, for compact $K\ni0$,
\[
|\langle\delta_0',\varphi\rangle|
= |\varphi'(0)| \le \sup_K |\varphi'|.
\]
Hence $\delta_0'$ has \textbf{order $1$}.

\[
\text{functions } \subset
\text{measures (order $0$)} \subset
\text{order $1$ distributions} \subset
\text{order $2$ distributions} \subset \cdots
\]

	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Order $0$: only $\sup|\varphi|$ matters (regular $T_f$, $\delta_x$).
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Order $1$: depends on first derivatives of $\varphi$ ($\delta'$, $\mathrm{P.V.}(1/x)$).
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Order $m$: depends on derivatives up to order $m$ — increasingly singular.

\clearpage

\subsection*{Exercise 2.7. Local mollification in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$}

Suppose $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)$.
Let $\phi_\varepsilon$ be the standard mollifier from Theorem~1.13 and,
for $x$ with $d(x,\partial\Omega)\ge\varepsilon$, define
\[
f_\varepsilon(x)
:= T_f\bigl[T_x \check{\phi}_\varepsilon\bigr],
\]
where $\check{\phi}_\varepsilon(y):=\phi_\varepsilon(-y)$ and
$T_x\psi(y):=\psi(y-x)$.

\medskip\noindent
Show that for any compact $K\subset\Omega$,
\[
\|f_\varepsilon - f\|_{L^1(K)} \longrightarrow 0
\qquad\text{as }\varepsilon\to0.
\]
\emph{Hint:} Follow the proof of Theorem~2.2, but use part~(b) of
Theorem~1.13.
\end{problem}

\begin{solution}
By part~(b), $D^\alpha T_f = T_{D^\alpha f}$. By part~(a),
	$a_\alpha T_{D^\alpha f} = T_{a_\alpha D^\alpha f}$, hence
	\[
	\sum_{|\alpha|\le k} a_\alpha D^\alpha T_f
	= \sum_{|\alpha|\le k} T_{a_\alpha D^\alpha f}
	= T_{\sum_{|\alpha|\le k} a_\alpha D^\alpha f}
	= T_{Lf}.
	\]
\end{solution}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0090]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0090}
\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Suppose $f \in H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^n)$ has compact support.
	Show that there exists a sequence $\varphi_j \in C_0^\infty(\mathbb{R}^n)$ such that
	\[
	\varphi_j \longrightarrow f \quad\text{in } H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^n).

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0092]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0092}
The line with two origins

Let
	\[
	X=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^2: y=\pm1\}
	\]
	with the subspace topology from $\mathbb{R}^2$, and define an equivalence relation on $X$ by
	\[
	(x,y)\sim(x',y') \iff x'=x\neq 0 \ \text{ and }\ y'=-y.
	\]
	Let $Q=X/\!\sim$ and $q:X\to Q$ be the quotient map. Denote
	\[
	o_+=q(0,1),\qquad o_-=q(0,-1).
	\]

	\subsection*{(a) Every point of $Q$ has a neighborhood homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}$}

	Fix $a\neq0$ and choose $\varepsilon>0$ so that $(a-\varepsilon,a+\varepsilon)$ does not meet $0$.
	Then
	\[
	U = q\big((a-\varepsilon,a+\varepsilon)\times\{1\}\big)
	= q\big((a-\varepsilon,a+\varepsilon)\times\{-1\}\big)
	\]
	and the map $t\mapsto q(t,1)$ is a homeomorphism $(a-\varepsilon,a+\varepsilon)\xrightarrow{\cong} U$.

	For small $\varepsilon>0$, define
	\[
	\phi_+:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\longrightarrow Q,\qquad
	\phi_+(t)=
	\begin{cases}
		q(t,1), & t\le 0,\\[2pt]
		q(t,-1), & t>0.
	\end{cases}
	\]
	Then $\phi_+$ is continuous, bijective onto $U_+=\phi_+((-\varepsilon,\varepsilon))$, and its inverse is continuous (it pulls back opens to unions of opens in the two lines). Hence $U_+\cong\mathbb{R}$.

	Define
	\[
	\phi_-:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to Q,\qquad
	\phi_-(t)=
	\begin{cases}
		q(t,-1), & t\le 0,\\[2pt]
		q(t,1),  & t>0.
	\end{cases}
	\]
	Then $U_-=\phi_-((-\varepsilon,\varepsilon))$ is a neighborhood of $o_-$ homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}$.

	Thus each point of $Q$ has a neighborhood homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}$.

	Let $U\ni o_+$ and $V\ni o_-$ be arbitrary open neighborhoods. By the constructions above,
	for all sufficiently small $t>0$ we have $q(t,1)=q(t,-1)\in U$ and also $q(t,1)=q(t,-1)\in V$.
	Hence $U\cap V\neq\varnothing$, so $o_+$ and $o_-$ cannot be separated by disjoint open sets.
	Therefore $Q$ is not Hausdorff.

	$Q$ is the classical ``line with two origins'': it is locally homeomorphic to $\mathbb{R}$, but it fails to be Hausdorff.

		\par\noindent \textbf{(10)}\quad $X=[0,1]\cup[2,3]$ with $1\sim2$.

			\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Classes: $\{x\}$ for every $x\in X\setminus\{1,2\}$, and the special class $\{1,2\}$.

		\par\noindent \textbf{(11b)}\quad $X\times Y$ with $(x,y)\sim(x',y')\iff x=x'$ (assume $Y\neq\varnothing$).

			\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Classes: for each $x\in X$, the vertical fiber $\{x\}\times Y$.

		\par\noindent \textbf{(12)}\quad $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $(x,y)\sim(x',y')\iff (x-x',y-y')\in\mathbb{Z}^2$.

			\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Classes: lattice cosets $(x,y)+\mathbb{Z}^2=\{(x+m,y+n):m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}$.
			\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Equivalently: one representative in $[0,1)\times[0,1)$, with opposite edges identified.

		\par\noindent \textbf{(13)}\quad $X=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^2:y=\pm1\}$ with $(x,y)\sim(x',y')\iff x=x'\neq0,\ y'=-y$.

			\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Classes: for $x\neq0$, the pair $\{(x,1),(x,-1)\}$; for $x=0$, the singletons $\{(0,1)\}$ and $\{(0,-1)\}$.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0100]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0100}
— Global diffeomorphism under $\|Df - I\| \le \mu < 1$

\textbf{Statement.}\quad

Let $f:\mathbb{R}^n \to \mathbb{R}^n$ be $C^1$ and assume
\[
\|Df(x) - I\| \le \mu \quad \text{for some } \mu\in(0,1) \text{ and all } x.
\]
Show:
 \setlength{\itemsep}{6pt}
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad $f$ maps open sets to open sets.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad For all $x,y$,
	\[
	(1-\mu)\,\|x-y\| \;\le\; \|f(x)-f(y)\| \;\le\; (1+\mu)\,\|x-y\|.
	\]
	Hence $f$ is injective and $f(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is closed.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Conclude that $f$ is a diffeomorphism of $\mathbb{R}^n$.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Explain why the conclusion can fail if one only assumes $\|Df(x)-I\|\le 1$.

\bigskip\hrule\bigskip

\textbf{Theory \& Solution.}\quad

\textbf{(Two–sided Lipschitz via line integration).}\;
Let $h:=y-x$ and $g(t):=f(x+th)$. Then
\[
f(y)-f(x)=\int_{0}^{1} Df(x+th)\,h\,dt
= h + \int_{0}^{1} (Df(x+th)-I)\,h\,dt.
\]
Hence
\[
\|f(y)-f(x)-h\|
\;\le\; \int_{0}^{1} \|Df(x+th)-I\|\,\|h\|\,dt
\;\le\; \mu\,\|h\|.
\]
By the triangle inequality,
\[
(1-\mu)\,\|h\|\ \le\ \|f(y)-f(x)\|\ \le\ (1+\mu)\,\|h\|.
\]
\emph{Injectivity} follows from the lower bound (if $f(x)=f(y)$ then $x=y$).

\medskip

\textbf{(Local diffeomorphism).}\;
Since $\|Df(x)-I\|<1$, each $Df(x)$ is invertible (Neumann series).
By the inverse function theorem, $f$ is a local $C^1$ diffeomorphism and therefore
\emph{maps open sets to open sets}.

\medskip

\textbf{(Closed range).}\;
If $f(x_k)\to y$ and $(x_k)$ is bounded, the lower Lipschitz bound gives
$\|x_k-x_\ell\|\le \tfrac{1}{1-\mu}\,\|f(x_k)-f(x_\ell)\|$, so $(x_k)$ is Cauchy
and converges to some $x$ with $f(x)=y$. Thus $f(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is closed.

\medskip

\textbf{(Surjectivity and global diffeo).}\;
The image $f(\mathbb{R}^n)$ is nonempty, open (local diffeo) and closed (previous step)
in the connected space $\mathbb{R}^n$, hence $f(\mathbb{R}^n)=\mathbb{R}^n$.
Thus $f$ is bijective; by the inverse function theorem $f^{-1}$ is $C^1$.
Therefore $f$ is a global $C^1$ \emph{diffeomorphism}.

\bigskip\hrule\bigskip

\textbf{Why not with “$\le 1$” only?}\quad

If $\|Df-I\|\le 1$, invertibility of $Df$ can fail somewhere.
In $n=1$, let $\phi$ be a $C^\infty$ bump with $0\le \phi \le 1$ and set
$f'(x)=1-\phi(x)$ so that $|f'(x)-1|\le 1$ but $f'(0)=0$.
Then $f$ is not locally invertible near $0$, and the two–sided Lipschitz bound can fail.

\bigskip\hrule\bigskip

\textbf{Examples.}\quad

 \setlength{\itemsep}{6pt}
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad \emph{Scalar case:} $f(x)=x-\tanh x$ on $\mathbb{R}$.
	Then $f'(x)=1-\operatorname{sech}^2 x\in(0,1)$, so $\|Df-I\|=\sup_x|f'(x)-1|<1$.
	The bounds give $(1-\mu)|x-y|\le|f(x)-f(y)|\le(1+\mu)|x-y|$,
	and $f$ is a global $C^1$ diffeomorphism of $\mathbb{R}$.

	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad \emph{Vector case:} $f(x)=x+g(x)$ with $\|Dg(x)\|\le \mu<1$ (e.g.\ $g(x)=\varepsilon\sin x$ componentwise).
	Then $\|Df-I\|\le\mu$ and all conclusions apply; $f$ is bi-Lipschitz and globally invertible.
\end{problem}

\begin{problem}[CP-II-0108]
\label{prob:cp-ii-0108}
Let $f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$ be uniformly continuous and suppose $\int_0^\infty f(x)\,dx<\infty$.

	\par\noindent (a)\quad Show that $\lim_{x\to\infty} f(x)=0$.
	\par\noindent (b)\quad Is the conclusion still true if $f$ is only continuous? Prove or give a counterexample.

Assume $\limsup_{x\to\infty} f(x)=\varepsilon_0>0$. Let $\varepsilon=\varepsilon_0/2$.
By uniform continuity, there exists $\eta>0$ such that
$|x-y|<\eta \Rightarrow |f(x)-f(y)|<\varepsilon$ for all $x,y\ge0$.
Choose $x_n\to\infty$ with $f(x_n)>\varepsilon_0$. Then for every
$y\in (x_n-\eta/2,\;x_n+\eta/2)$ we have
\[
f(y)\ge f(x_n)-\varepsilon>\varepsilon_0/2.
\]
Taking a subsequence so that these intervals are pairwise disjoint, we obtain
\[
\int_0^\infty f(x)\,dx
\ \ge\ \sum_n \int_{x_n-\eta/2}^{x_n+\eta/2} f(x)\,dx
\ \ge\ \sum_n \frac{\eta}{2}\cdot \frac{\varepsilon_0}{2}
\ =\ \infty,
\]
a contradiction. Hence $\lim_{x\to\infty} f(x)=0$.

For $n\in\mathbb{N}$ define a triangular bump
\[
\phi_n(x)=\max\!\Big(1-\frac{|x-n|}{w_n},\,0\Big),\qquad w_n=2^{-n},
\]
and set $f(x)=\sum_{n=1}^\infty \phi_n(x)$. The supports are disjoint, so $f$ is continuous and
\[
\int_0^\infty f(x)\,dx=\sum_{n=1}^\infty \int \phi_n(x)\,dx
=\sum_{n=1}^\infty w_n=\sum_{n=1}^\infty 2^{-n}<\infty.
\]
But $f(n)=1$ for all $n$, hence $\limsup_{x\to\infty} f(x)\ge 1$ and the limit at infinity does not exist (in particular, it is not $0$).
Therefore the conclusion fails if we assume only continuity.

\begin{center}\Large\bfseries $\ell_\infty$ (sup metric) is complete \quad+\quad $\ell_2$ (Euclidean metric) is a metric \& complete\end{center}

(a) Let $\ell_\infty$ be the space of all bounded real sequences.
Equip it with the metric
\[
d_\infty((x_n),(y_n))=\sup_{n\in\mathbb N}|x_n-y_n|.
\]
Show that $(\ell_\infty,d_\infty)$ is complete.

\medskip
(b) Let
\[
\ell_2=\Big\{(x_n)\in\mathbb R^{\mathbb N}:\sum_{n=1}^\infty x_n^2<\infty\Big\}
\]
and define
\[
d((x_n),(y_n))=\Big(\sum_{n=1}^\infty (x_n-y_n)^2\Big)^{1/2}.
\]
Show that $d$ is a well-defined metric on $\ell_2$ and that $(\ell_2,d)$ is complete.

\medskip\medskip
\textbf{Theory (used).}

	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad A metric space is complete iff every Cauchy sequence converges in the space.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad In $\mathbb R$, every Cauchy sequence converges (completeness of $\mathbb R$).
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad If $x,y\in\ell_2$, then $(x_n-y_n)^2\le 2x_n^2+2y_n^2$, hence $x-y\in\ell_2$.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Minkowski inequality: $\|u+v\|_2\le \|u\|_2+\|v\|_2$.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Fast–Cauchy subsequence trick: from a Cauchy sequence choose a subsequence with
	$\|x_{j+1}-x_j\|\le 2^{-j}$ so the telescoping series converges.

\medskip\medskip
\textbf{Solution.}

\textbf{(a) Completeness of $\ell_\infty$.}
Let $(x^{(k)})_{k\ge1}$ be Cauchy in $d_\infty$.

For fixed $n$ we have
\[
|x^{(k)}_n-x^{(m)}_n|\le d_\infty(x^{(k)},x^{(m)}),
\]
so $(x^{(k)}_n)$ is Cauchy in $\mathbb R$ and thus converges.
Set
\[
x_n:=\lim_{k\to\infty}x^{(k)}_n,
\qquad
x:=(x_n)_{n\ge1}.
\]

Choose $k_0$ with
\[
d_\infty(x^{(k)},x^{(k_0)})\le 1
\quad\text{for all }k\ge k_0.
\]
Then for all $n$,
\[
|x_n|\le |x^{(k_0)}_n|+|x_n-x^{(k_0)}_n|
\le \|x^{(k_0)}\|_\infty+1,
\]
hence $x\in\ell_\infty$.

Let $\varepsilon>0$ and pick $K$ so that
\[
d_\infty(x^{(k)},x^{(m)})<\varepsilon/2
\quad\text{for all }k,m\ge K.
\]
Fix $k\ge K$ and let $m\to\infty$.
Then for every $n$,
\[
|x^{(k)}_n-x_n|\le \varepsilon/2.
\]
Taking suprema,
\[
\|x^{(k)}-x\|_\infty\le \varepsilon/2,
\]
so $x^{(k)}\to x$ in $\ell_\infty$.
Therefore $(\ell_\infty,d_\infty)$ is complete.

\medskip
\textbf{(b) $d$ is a metric on $\ell_2$ and $\ell_2$ is complete.}

\emph{Well-definedness.}
For $x,y\in\ell_2$,
\[
(x_n-y_n)^2\le 2x_n^2+2y_n^2,
\]
hence $\sum (x_n-y_n)^2<\infty$ and $d(x,y)<\infty$.

\emph{Metric axioms.}
Nonnegativity and symmetry are clear.
If $d(x,y)=0$ then $\sum (x_n-y_n)^2=0$ and thus $x_n=y_n$ for all $n$.
Minkowski gives the triangle inequality:
\[
\|x-z\|_2=\|(x-y)+(y-z)\|_2
\le \|x-y\|_2+\|y-z\|_2.
\]

\emph{Completeness.}
Let $(x^{(k)})$ be Cauchy in $\ell_2$.
Choose a subsequence $(x^{(k_j)})$ with
\[
\|x^{(k_{j+1})}-x^{(k_j)}\|_2\le 2^{-j}.
\]
Put $u^{(j)}:=x^{(k_{j+1})}-x^{(k_j)}$.
Then
\[
\sum_{j=1}^\infty \|u^{(j)}\|_2<\infty,
\]
so $\sum_{j=1}^\infty u^{(j)}$ converges in $\ell_2$ to some $v$.
Define
\[
x^{(\infty)}:=x^{(k_1)}+v \in \ell_2.
\]
Then $x^{(k_j)}\to x^{(\infty)}$ in $\ell_2$.

Since $(x^{(k)})$ is Cauchy, given $\varepsilon>0$ we can choose
$J$ and $N$ so that
\[
\|x^{(k_J)}-x^{(\infty)}\|_2<\varepsilon/2
\quad\text{and}\quad
\|x^{(k)}-x^{(m)}\|_2<\varepsilon/2
\ \text{for }k,m\ge N.
\]
For $k\ge \max\{N,k_J\}$,
\[
\|x^{(k)}-x^{(\infty)}\|_2
\le \|x^{(k)}-x^{(k_J)}\|_2+\|x^{(k_J)}-x^{(\infty)}\|_2
<\varepsilon.
\]
Hence $x^{(k)}\to x^{(\infty)}\in\ell_2$.
Therefore $(\ell_2,d)$ is complete.

\medskip\medskip
\textbf{Examples.}

\emph{In $\ell_\infty$:}
The unit vectors $e^{(k)}$ satisfy
\[
\|e^{(k)}-e^{(m)}\|_\infty=1
\quad (k\ne m),
\]
so they are not Cauchy.
But for
\[
x^{(k)}=(\underbrace{1/k,\dots,1/k}_{k\ \text{terms}},0,0,\dots)
\]
we have
\[
\|x^{(k)}\|_\infty=1/k\to0.
\]

\medskip
\emph{In $\ell_2$:}
For the same $x^{(k)}$,
\[
\|x^{(k)}\|_2^2=k\cdot(1/k^2)=1/k\to0,
\]
so $x^{(k)}\to0$ in $\ell_2$,
while
\[
\|e^{(k)}-e^{(m)}\|_2=\sqrt2
\quad (k\ne m)
\]
shows $(e^{(k)})$ is not Cauchy.

On a nonempty complete metric space $(X,d)$, let $f:X\to X$ be continuous and suppose that
for each $x\neq y$ there is $L(x,y)\in(0,1)$ with
\[
d\big(f(x),f(y)\big)\ \le\ L(x,y)\, d(x,y).
\]

	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Show that, if $f$ has a fixed point, then it is unique.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Must $f$ be a contraction? Must $f$ have a fixed point?
	Give a proof or counterexample.
	\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Show that, if $(X,d)$ is assumed to be compact,
	then $f$ necessarily has a unique fixed point.

\medskip\medskip
\textbf{Theory.}

A map with $d(fx,fy)<d(x,y)$ for all $x\neq y$ is a \emph{strict contraction} (Edelstein).
Such a map is injective and has at most one fixed point.
On a compact metric space every strict contraction has a fixed point.

\medskip\medskip
\textbf{Solution.}

\emph{(a) Uniqueness.}
If $p,q$ are fixed, then for $p\neq q$,
\[
d(p,q)=d(f(p),f(q))\le L(p,q)\,d(p,q),
\]
which forces $d(p,q)=0$. Hence $p=q$.

\medskip
\emph{(b) Not necessarily Banach.}
On $\mathbb R$ with the usual metric let $f(x)=\log\cosh x$.
Then by the mean value theorem
\[
|f(x)-f(y)|=|\tanh c|\,|x-y|<|x-y|
\]
for some $c$ between $x$ and $y$.
Thus the hypothesis holds, but $\sup|\tanh|=1$ so no single $L<1$ works.

\emph{(b) No fixed point in general.}
Define a strictly increasing $C^1$ bijection $\phi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$
with strictly decreasing derivative $\phi'>0$ (e.g. $\phi'(t)=\tfrac12+\frac{1}{1+e^{t}}$).
Let
\[
d(x,y)=|\phi(x)-\phi(y)| \qquad (x,y\in\mathbb R).
\]
Then $(\mathbb R,d)$ is complete.
For $T(x)=x+1$ and $x\neq y$,
\[
\frac{d(Tx,Ty)}{d(x,y)}
=\frac{\phi'(c_2)}{\phi'(c_1)}<1
\]
for suitable $c_1<c_2$, since $\phi'$ is decreasing.
Hence $T$ satisfies the hypothesis but $T$ has no fixed point.

\medskip
\emph{(c) Compact case.}
Let $x_{n+1}=f(x_n)$. By compactness, a subsequence $x_{n_k}\to x^\star$ exists.
Strict contraction implies the whole $(x_n)$ is Cauchy, hence $x_n\to x^\star$.
By continuity,
\[
f(x^\star)=\lim_{n\to\infty} f(x_n)=\lim_{n\to\infty} x_{n+1}=x^\star.
\]
Uniqueness follows from (a).


\medskip\medskip
\textbf{Examples.}

(1) $f(x)=\log\cosh x$ on $(\mathbb R,|\cdot|)$:
\ $|f(x)-f(y)|=|\tanh c|\,|x-y|$ with $|\tanh c|<1$ but no uniform $L<1$.

(2) On $(\mathbb R,d)$ with $d(x,y)=|\phi(x)-\phi(y)|$ as above,
the shift $x\mapsto x+1$ is a strict contraction and has no fixed point.

\noindent\Large\bfseries Banach contraction, extraction, and Edelstein\par\normalfont\normalsize

\medskip
\textbf{Banach contraction principle.}
Let $(X,d)$ be complete and let $f:X\to X$ satisfy
\[
d(f(x),f(y)) \le L\, d(x,y)\qquad (0<L<1,\ \forall x,y\in X).
\]
Then $f$ has a unique fixed point $x^{*}$ and, for any start $x_{0}$, the iterates $x_{n+1}=f(x_{n})$ converge to $x^{*}$ with
\[
d(x_{n},x^{*}) \le \frac{L^{n}}{1-L}\, d(x_{1},x_{0}).
\]

\medskip
\textit{Extraction (fast Cauchy subsequence).}
From any Cauchy $(x^{k})$ choose $k_{1}<k_{2}<\cdots$ so that
\[
d\big(x^{k_{j+1}},x^{k_{j}}\big)\le 2^{-j}.
\]
Then $\sum_{j} d(x^{k_{j+1}},x^{k_{j}})<\infty$, hence $(x^{k_{j}})$ is Cauchy and converges in a complete space; the original sequence has the same limit.

\medskip
\textbf{Edelstein (strict contraction on compact spaces).}
If $(X,d)$ is compact and
\[
d(f(x),f(y))<d(x,y)\qquad (\forall x\neq y),
\]
then $f$ has a unique fixed point $x^{*}$ and $x_{n+1}=f(x_{n})\to x^{*}$ for any $x_{0}$.

\smallskip
\textit{Sketch.}
For $m>n$,
\[
d(x_{m+1},x_{n+1})=d\big(f(x_{m}),f(x_{n})\big)<d(x_{m},x_{n}),
\]
so orbit distances strictly decrease.
Compactness gives a convergent subsequence $x_{n_{k}}\to x^{*}$; strict decrease forces every subsequence to share the same limit, hence $(x_{n})$ is Cauchy and convergent to $x^{*}$. Continuity yields $f(x^{*})=x^{*}$; uniqueness is immediate.

\medskip
\begin{table}[h]
	\centering
	\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{} l X X @{}}
		\toprule
		& \textbf{Banach} & \textbf{Edelstein} \\
		\midrule
		Hypothesis & Global Lipschitz constant $L<1$ for all pairs &
		Strict shrinkage $d(fx,fy)<d(x,y)$ (local ratios may approach $1$) \\
		Space needed & Complete metric space & Compact metric space \\
		Conclusions & Unique fixed point; Picard iterates converge with linear rate and error bound &
		Unique fixed point; Picard iterates converge (no uniform rate guaranteed) \\
		Typical use & Contraction mappings, iterative solvers with known $L$ &
		Maps that shrink but lack a global $L<1$; rely on compactness \\
		\bottomrule
	\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\medskip
\textbf{Examples.}
(1) \emph{Edelstein but not Banach on $(\mathbb R,|\cdot|)$.} $f(x)=\log(\cosh x)$.
Mean Value Theorem: $|f(x)-f(y)|=|f'(c)|\,|x-y|=|\tanh c|\,|x-y|<|x-y|$, but $\sup_{t}|\tanh t|=1$ (no single $L<1$).

(2) \emph{Strict but no fixed point without compactness.}
Let $\phi:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ be $C^{1}$, strictly increasing, with strictly decreasing $\phi'>0$, and set $d(x,y)=|\phi(x)-\phi(y)|$.
Then $(\mathbb R,d)$ is complete; for $T(x)=x+1$,
\[
\frac{d(Tx,Ty)}{d(x,y)}=\frac{\phi'(c_{2})}{\phi'(c_{1})}<1
\]
for some $c_{1}\in(x,y)$ and $c_{2}\in(x+1,y+1)$, yet $T$ has no fixed point.

Let $0<a,b<\infty$ and let $f:[-a,a]\times[-b,b]\to\mathbb R$ be continuous.

\medskip

Consider the initial value problem
\[
\begin{cases}
	y'(x)=f\big(x,y(x)\big),\\[2pt]
	y(0)=y_0,
\end{cases}
\qquad (x \text{ near } 0).
\]

\medskip

(a)\; Is the solution necessarily unique? Give a proof or a counterexample.

\medskip

(b)\; For every $k\in\mathbb N$ show there is an interval $I_k\ni0$ and $y_k\in C(I_k)$ solving
\[
\begin{cases}
	y_k'(x)=f_k\big(x,y_k(x)\big),\\[2pt]
	y_k(0)=y_0,
\end{cases}
\]
where $f_k$ are Lipschitz on $[-a,a]\times[-b,b]$ and $f_k\to f$ uniformly.

\medskip

What happens as $k\to\infty$?

\medskip

(c)\; Show there is $\varepsilon\in(0,a]$ and a solution $y$ on $(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)$ of the original IVP.

\medskip

Compare with (b).

\medskip

\textbf{Theory.}

Picard--Lindel\"of: Lipschitz in $y$ $\Rightarrow$ unique local solution.

\medskip

Peano: continuity $\Rightarrow$ local solution (not necessarily unique).

\medskip

Arzel\`a--Ascoli: uniformly bounded and equicontinuous on a compact interval $\Rightarrow$ a uniformly convergent subsequence.

\medskip

Integral form: if $y_k(x)=y_0+\int_0^x f_k(t,y_k(t))\,dt$ and $y_k\to y$, $f_k\to f$ uniformly, then $y(x)=y_0+\int_0^x f(t,y(t))\,dt$.

\medskip

\textbf{Solution.}

\emph{(a) Nonuniqueness in general.}

Take $y'(x)=\sqrt{|y(x)|}$, $y(0)=0$.

Then $y\equiv0$ and $y(x)=\tfrac{x^2}{4}$ for $x\ge0$ (patched with $0$ for $x\le0$)
are distinct solutions.

Hence continuity alone does not guarantee uniqueness.

\medskip

\emph{(b) Lipschitz approximations; behaviour as $k\to\infty$.}

Since $f$ is continuous on the compact rectangle $R=[-a,a]\times[-b,b]$,
there exists $M$ with $|f|\le M$ on $R$.

Choose Lipschitz $f_k:R\to\mathbb R$ with $f_k\to f$ uniformly.

By Picard--Lindel\"of, each IVP with $f_k$ has a unique solution $y_k$ on a maximal interval $I_k\ni0$.

\medskip

Let $\varepsilon:=\min\{a,\ b/M\}$ (interpret $b/M=\infty$ if $M=0$).

Then, for $|x|\le\varepsilon$,
\[
|y_k(x)-y_0|\le \int_0^x |f_k(t,y_k(t))|\,dt \le M|x|\le b,
\]
so $y_k$ is defined on the common interval $I=(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)$ and takes values in $[-b,b]$.

\medskip

Moreover, for $x,t\in I$,
\[
|y_k(x)-y_k(t)|\le \int_t^x |f_k(s,y_k(s))|\,ds \le M|x-t|,
\]
hence $\{y_k\}$ is uniformly bounded and equicontinuous on $\overline I$.

\medskip

By Arzel\`a--Ascoli there is a subsequence $y_{k_j}\to y$ uniformly on $\overline I$.

Passing to the limit in
\[
y_{k_j}(x)=y_0+\int_0^x f_{k_j}\big(t,y_{k_j}(t)\big)\,dt
\]
gives
\[
y(x)=y_0+\int_0^x f\big(t,y(t)\big)\,dt,
\]
so $y$ solves the original IVP on $I$.

\medskip

As $k\to\infty$, at least a subsequence $y_{k_j}$ converges to a solution of the original problem.

If the original problem is unique, then $y_k\to y$.

If it is nonunique, different subsequences (or different choices of $f_k$) may converge to different solutions.

\medskip

\emph{(c) Existence on $(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)$.}

The construction in (b) proves Peano existence: there exists a solution
$y$ on $I=(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)$ with
\[
y(x)=y_0+\int_0^x f\big(t,y(t)\big)\,dt,\qquad |x|<\varepsilon.
\]

Compared with (b): approximating by Lipschitz $f_k$ (unique solvability) and compactness
yield a convergent subsequence and hence existence for the original continuous $f$,
but uniqueness need not hold.

On a normed vector space there is a canonical metric
$d(x,y)=\|x-y\|$.

Show that on a finite-dimensional real vector space, every choice of norm (and thus canonical metric) is equivalent.

Is this still true for infinite-dimensional vector spaces?

\medskip
\textbf{Theory.}

Two norms $\|\cdot\|_a,\|\cdot\|_b$ on $V$ are equivalent iff
there exist constants $m,M>0$ with
\[
m\|x\|_b \le \|x\|_a \le M\|x\|_b \qquad \text{for all } x\in V.
\]
Finite-dimensional normed spaces have compact unit spheres for any norm.
Continuous functions on compact sets attain positive minima and finite maxima.

\medskip
\textbf{Solution.}

\emph{Finite dimension $\Rightarrow$ all norms are equivalent.}

Let $\|\cdot\|_a$ and $\|\cdot\|_b$ be norms on a finite-dimensional real space $V$.

Consider the $b$-unit sphere $S_b=\{x\in V:\|x\|_b=1\}$, which is compact.

The map $x\mapsto\|x\|_a$ is continuous, hence attains
\[
m:=\min_{x\in S_b}\|x\|_a>0,
\qquad
M:=\max_{x\in S_b}\|x\|_a<\infty.
\]
For any $x\neq 0$, write $x=\|x\|_b\,u$ with $u\in S_b$. Then
\[
m\|x\|_b \le \|x\|_a = \|x\|_b\,\|u\|_a \le M\|x\|_b.
\]
The inequalities also hold for $x=0$. Therefore the two norms are equivalent.

\medskip
\emph{Infinite dimension $\Rightarrow$ not all norms are equivalent.}

Take $V=C[0,1]$ and define
\[
\|f\|_\infty=\max_{t\in[0,1]}|f(t)|,
\qquad
\|f\|_1=\int_0^1 |f(t)|\,dt.
\]
Let $(f_n)$ be continuous functions with $\|f_n\|_\infty=1$ and
$\|f_n\|_1\to 0$ (e.g., triangular bumps of height $1$ and width $1/n$).
If $\|\cdot\|_\infty$ and $\|\cdot\|_1$ were equivalent, there would exist $m>0$
with $m\|f\|_\infty\le \|f\|_1$ for all $f\in V$, which is contradicted by $(f_n)$.
Hence these norms are \emph{not} equivalent.

\medskip
\textbf{Examples.}

On $\mathbb R^n$ one has the standard estimates
\[
\|x\|_\infty \le \|x\|_2 \le \|x\|_1 \le \sqrt{n}\,\|x\|_2 \le n\,\|x\|_\infty,
\]
so $\|\cdot\|_1,\|\cdot\|_2,\|\cdot\|_\infty$ are equivalent.

On $C[0,1]$, the sequence $(f_n)$ above converges to $0$ in $\|\cdot\|_1$
but not in $\|\cdot\|_\infty$, showing non-equivalence of the norms.

\textbf{Theory (what we’ll use).}

\emph{Picard--Lindel\"of (Banach).}
If $f$ is continuous on $R=[-a,a]\times[-b,b]$ and Lipschitz in $y$ with constant $L$,
then for any $y_0\in[-b,b]$ there exists
\[
\varepsilon\le \min\Big\{a,\ \frac{b}{M},\ \frac{1}{L}\Big\},\qquad M=\sup_R |f|,
\]
such that the IVP $y'(x)=f(x,y(x))$, $y(0)=y_0$ has a unique solution on $[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]$.
It is the unique fixed point of $T:C([-\varepsilon,\varepsilon])\to C([-\varepsilon,\varepsilon])$,
\[
(Ty)(x)=y_0+\int_0^x f(t,y(t))\,dt,
\]
which is a contraction when $L\varepsilon<1$.

\medskip
\emph{Peano existence.}
If $f$ is merely continuous on $R$, then there exists
$\varepsilon\le \min\{a,b/M\}$ and a (not necessarily unique) solution on $[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]$.
Proof can be done via polygonal approximations or by Lipschitz approximants $f_k\to f$.

\medskip
\emph{Uniform bounds / domain control.}
If $|f|\le M$ on $R$, any solution satisfies
\[
|y(x)-y_0|\le M|x| \quad \text{for } |x|\le \varepsilon,
\]
hence $y([-\varepsilon,\varepsilon])\subset[-b,b]$ whenever $\varepsilon\le b/M$.

\medskip
\emph{Arzel\`a--Ascoli.}
A uniformly bounded and equicontinuous family in $C([c,d])$ has a uniformly convergent subsequence.
In particular, if $|y_k'|\le M$ a.e., then $|y_k(x)-y_k(t)|\le M|x-t|$ (equicontinuity).

\medskip
\emph{Passing to the limit in integral form.}
If $y_k\to y$ uniformly on $[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon]$, $f_k\to f$ uniformly on $R$,
and $y_k([- \varepsilon,\varepsilon])\subset[-b,b]$, then
\[
\sup_{x}\big|f_k(x,y_k(x))-f(x,y(x))\big|\to0,
\]
so from
\[
y_k(x)=y_0+\int_0^x f_k(t,y_k(t))\,dt
\]
we obtain
\[
y(x)=y_0+\int_0^x f(t,y(t))\,dt,
\]
and hence $y'(x)=f(x,y(x))$ for all $x$.

The goal is to guide you through a proof of the Stone–Weierstrass (Bernstein) theorem in one variable:
every continuous $f:K\to\mathbb R$ on a rectangle
$K=[a_1,b_1]\times\cdots\times[a_n,b_n]\subset\mathbb R^n$
can be uniformly approximated by polynomials.
In what follows we take $n=1$.

\medskip
(a)\; Show that, without loss of generality, it is enough to treat $[a_1,b_1]=[0,1]$.

\medskip
(b)\; For $k=0,\dots,n$ let
\[
p_{n,k}(x)=\binom{n}{k}\,x^{k}(1-x)^{\,n-k}.
\]
Show that
\[
\sum_{k=0}^{n} p_{n,k}(x)=1,
\qquad
\sum_{k=0}^{n}\!\left(x-\frac{k}{n}\right)^{\!2} p_{n,k}(x)=\frac{x(1-x)}{n}.
\]

\medskip
(c)\; Define the Bernstein polynomial
\[
p_n(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n} f\!\left(\frac{k}{n}\right)\,p_{n,k}(x).
\]
Using the identity from (b), show that, for any $\delta>0$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
	|p_n(x)-f(x)|
	&\le
	\sum_{\;|x-\frac{k}{n}|<\delta} \bigl|f(\tfrac{k}{n})-f(x)\bigr|\,p_{n,k}(x)
	\;+\;
	\sum_{\;|x-\frac{k}{n}|\ge \delta} \bigl|f(\tfrac{k}{n})-f(x)\bigr|\,p_{n,k}(x).
\end{aligned}
\]

\medskip
(d)\; Choose $\delta$ appropriately and use the second identity in (b) to conclude that
$p_n \xrightarrow{\ \mathrm{unif}\ } f$ on $[0,1]$.

\medskip
\textbf{Theory (tools).}

\textbf{Affine reduction to $[0,1]$.}
For $a<b$, the map $\phi:[0,1]\to[a,b]$, $\phi(t)=a+(b-a)t$, is a homeomorphism.
If $g=f\circ\phi$ can be uniformly approximated on $[0,1]$ by polynomials $q_m$, then
$q_m\circ\phi^{-1}$ uniformly approximates $f$ on $[a,b]$.
Hence it suffices to work on $[0,1]$.

\medskip
\textbf{Bernstein basis identities.}
For $p_{n,k}(x)=\binom{n}{k}x^k(1-x)^{n-k}$,
\[
\begin{aligned}
	\sum_{k=0}^{n} p_{n,k}(x) &= 1,\\[4pt]
	\sum_{k=0}^{n} \frac{k}{n}\,p_{n,k}(x) &= x,\\[4pt]
	\sum_{k=0}^{n}\!\left(\frac{k}{n}-x\right)^{\!2} p_{n,k}(x) &= \frac{x(1-x)}{n}.
\end{aligned}
\]
(These are normalization, mean, and variance for a $\mathrm{Bin}(n,x)$ law.)

\medskip
\textbf{Bernstein operator.}
\[
B_nf(x):=\sum_{k=0}^{n} f(k/n)\,p_{n,k}(x),
\]
a polynomial of degree $\le n$.

\medskip
\textbf{Modulus of continuity.}
For $f\in C([0,1])$ define
\[
\omega_f(\delta)
:=\sup\{\,|f(u)-f(v)|:\ u,v\in[0,1],\ |u-v|\le\delta\,\},\qquad \delta\ge0.
\]
Then $\omega_f(\delta)\to0$ as $\delta\downarrow0$ (uniform continuity on a compact interval).

\medskip
\textbf{Error splitting and tail estimate.}
For any $\delta>0$,
\[
|B_nf(x)-f(x)|
\le \omega_f(\delta)
\;+\; 2\|f\|_\infty \sum_{|x-\frac{k}{n}|\ge\delta} p_{n,k}(x).
\]
By Chebyshev’s inequality together with the variance identity,
\[
\sum_{|x-\frac{k}{n}|\ge\delta} p_{n,k}(x)
\le \frac{1}{\delta^2}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\!\left(x-\frac{k}{n}\right)^{\!2} p_{n,k}(x)
= \frac{x(1-x)}{n\delta^2}
\le \frac{1}{4n\delta^2}.
\]
Hence
\[
\boxed{\;
	\|B_nf-f\|_\infty
	\le \omega_f(\delta) + \frac{\|f\|_\infty}{2n\delta^2}.
	\;}
\]
Given $\varepsilon>0$, first choose $\delta>0$ so that $\omega_f(\delta)<\varepsilon/2$,
then pick $n$ large with $\frac{\|f\|_\infty}{2n\delta^2}<\varepsilon/2$.
Therefore $B_nf\to f$ uniformly on $[0,1]$.

	\textbf{Solution.}

	\emph{(a) Reduction to $[0,1]$.}
	Let $a<b$ and $\phi:[0,1]\to[a,b]$, $\phi(t)=a+(b-a)t$.
	If $g=f\circ\phi$ is uniformly approximated on $[0,1]$ by polynomials $q_n$,
	then $q_n\circ\phi^{-1}$ are polynomials on $[a,b]$ and
	$\|f-(q_n\circ\phi^{-1})\|_{[a,b]}=\|g-q_n\|_{[0,1]}\to0$.
	Thus it suffices to work on $[0,1]$.

	\medskip
	\emph{(b) Identities for $p_{n,k}$.}
	For $p_{n,k}(x)=\binom{n}{k}x^k(1-x)^{n-k}$,
	\[
	\sum_{k=0}^{n} p_{n,k}(x)=(x+(1-x))^n=1.
	\]
	Let $X\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,x)$. Then $p_{n,k}(x)=\mathbb P[X=k]$ and hence
	\[
	\sum_{k=0}^{n}\Big(x-\frac{k}{n}\Big)^{2}p_{n,k}(x)
	=\mathbb E\Big[\Big(x-\frac{X}{n}\Big)^2\Big]
	=\mathrm{Var}\!\Big(\frac{X}{n}\Big)
	=\frac{1}{n^2}\mathrm{Var}(X)
	=\frac{x(1-x)}{n}.
	\]
	(Equivalently, use $\sum k\,p_{n,k}=nx$ and $\sum k^2p_{n,k}=n x(1-x)+n^2x^2$.)

	\medskip
	\emph{(c) Error splitting.}
	With $p_n(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n} f(k/n)\,p_{n,k}(x)$ we have
	\[
	p_n(x)-f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\big(f(\tfrac{k}{n})-f(x)\big)p_{n,k}(x).
	\]
	Thus, for any $\delta>0$,
	\[
	\begin{aligned}
		|p_n(x)-f(x)|
		&\le
		\sum_{\;|x-\frac{k}{n}|<\delta}\! \bigl|f(\tfrac{k}{n})-f(x)\bigr|\,p_{n,k}(x)
		\;+\;
		\sum_{\;|x-\frac{k}{n}|\ge \delta}\! \bigl|f(\tfrac{k}{n})-f(x)\bigr|\,p_{n,k}(x).
	\end{aligned}
	\]

	\medskip
	\emph{(d) Uniform convergence.}
	Let $\omega_f(\delta)=\sup\{|f(u)-f(v)|:\ |u-v|\le\delta\}$.
	Then $\omega_f(\delta)\to0$ as $\delta\downarrow0$.
	The first sum in (c) is $\le \omega_f(\delta)$ (convex combination).
	For the second sum, use $|f(\tfrac{k}{n})-f(x)|\le 2\|f\|_\infty$ and Chebyshev together with (b):
	\[
	\sum_{|x-\frac{k}{n}|\ge\delta} p_{n,k}(x)
	\le \frac{1}{\delta^2}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\Big(x-\frac{k}{n}\Big)^2 p_{n,k}(x)
	= \frac{x(1-x)}{n\delta^2}
	\le \frac{1}{4n\delta^2}.
	\]
	Hence
	\[
	\|p_n-f\|_\infty \le \omega_f(\delta) + \frac{\|f\|_\infty}{2n\delta^2}.
	\]
	Given $\varepsilon>0$, choose $\delta>0$ so that $\omega_f(\delta)<\varepsilon/2$,
	then choose $n$ large with $\frac{\|f\|_\infty}{2n\delta^2}<\varepsilon/2$.
	Therefore $p_n\to f$ uniformly on $[0,1]$.

	Let $(X,d)$ be a compact metric space, and let $C(X)$ denote the space of all real-valued continuous functions on $X$.
	We say that $F\subset C(X)$ is \emph{equicontinuous} if for every $x\in X$ and every $\varepsilon>0$ there exists a neighbourhood $U_x$ of $x$ such that
	\[
	|f(x)-f(y)|<\varepsilon \quad \text{for all } y\in U_x \text{ and all } f\in F.
	\]
	(The neighbourhood $U_x$ is the same for the entire family $F$.)

	Show that $F\subset C(X)$ is compact (for the uniform metric) if and only if it is \emph{closed}, \emph{bounded}, and \emph{equicontinuous}.

	\medskip
	(a)\; Using results from the lectures, justify that it is enough to show that $F\subset C(X)$ is \emph{totally bounded} if and only if it is \emph{bounded} and \emph{equicontinuous}.

	\medskip
	(b)\; Prove the $(\Rightarrow)$ direction: if $F\subset C(X)$ is totally bounded, then it is bounded and equicontinuous.

	\medskip
	(c)\; $(\star)$ Prove the $(\Leftarrow)$ direction. \emph{Hint:} use compactness of $X$ to construct a finite subfamily that is a candidate $\varepsilon$-net for $F$.

	\medskip
	(d)\; Deduce that if $(f_n)\subset C([a,b])$ is bounded and equicontinuous (for the uniform metric), then there exists a subsequence $(f_{n_k})$ that converges uniformly on $[a,b]$.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Theory (tools).}

	\textbf{Uniform metric and completeness.}
	On $C(X)$ use $d_\infty(f,g)=\|f-g\|_\infty=\sup_{x\in X}|f(x)-g(x)|$.
	If $X$ is compact then $(C(X),\|\cdot\|_\infty)$ is complete.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Compact = complete + totally bounded (metric spaces).}
	A subset of a metric space is compact iff it is complete and totally bounded.
	A closed subset of a complete space is complete.
	Hence it suffices to prove:
	\[
	\boxed{\text{$F$ totally bounded}\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \text{$F$ bounded and equicontinuous.}}
	\]

	\medskip
	\textbf{Totally bounded $\Rightarrow$ bounded \& equicontinuous.}
	Let $\varepsilon>0$ and choose a finite $\varepsilon/3$-net $\{g_1,\dots,g_m\}\subset F$ in $\|\cdot\|_\infty$.
	Each $g_i$ is uniformly continuous on compact $X$; choose $\delta>0$ that works for all $i$.
	If $d(x,y)<\delta$ and $f\in F$, pick $i$ with $\|f-g_i\|_\infty<\varepsilon/3$; then
	\[
	|f(x)-f(y)|\le 2\|f-g_i\|_\infty+|g_i(x)-g_i(y)|<\varepsilon.
	\]
	Boundedness follows since a finite $\varepsilon$-net of $F$ is bounded and hence so is $F$.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Bounded \& equicontinuous $\Rightarrow$ totally bounded (Arzel\`a–Ascoli).}
	Fix $\varepsilon>0$.
	Equicontinuity gives $\delta>0$ with $d(x,y)<\delta\Rightarrow |f(x)-f(y)|<\varepsilon/3$ for all $f\in F$.

	Cover $X$ by finitely many balls $B(x_j,\delta)$, $j=1,\dots,N$.
	If $|f|\le M$ on $X$ for all $f\in F$, partition $[-M,M]$ into intervals of length $\varepsilon/3$.

	For each $f\in F$ choose grid points $a_j$ with $|f(x_j)-a_j|\le \varepsilon/6$ and define a template function that on $B(x_j,\delta)$ takes value $a_j$.

	This finite template family is an $\varepsilon$-net for $F$, hence $F$ is totally bounded.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Sequential consequence.}
	On $[a,b]$, any bounded, equicontinuous family in $C([a,b])$ has a uniformly convergent subsequence (Arzel\`a–Ascoli).

	\textbf{Equicontinuity is essential: counterexamples and remarks.}

	\medskip
	\textbf{Setup.}
	Let $(X,d)$ be a metric space and $C(X)$ the space of real--valued continuous
	functions on $X$ with the uniform norm $\|f\|_\infty=\sup_{x\in X}|f(x)|$.
	A family $F\subset C(X)$ is \emph{equicontinuous} if
	\[
	\forall\,x\in X\ \forall\,\varepsilon>0\ \exists\,\delta>0\ \text{s.t.}\
	d(x,y)<\delta\ \Longrightarrow\ |f(x)-f(y)|<\varepsilon\quad \text{for all } f\in F.
	\]
	Equivalently, when $X$ is compact, for every $\varepsilon>0$ there exists a \emph{global}
	$\delta>0$ that works for all $x,y\in X$ and all $f\in F$.

	\medskip
	\textbf{(1) Bounded but not equicontinuous $\Rightarrow$ not relatively compact.}
	Let $X=[0,1]$ and
	\[
	F=\{\,f_n(x)=x^n:\ n\in\mathbb N\,\}\subset C([0,1]).
	\]
	Then $\|f_n\|_\infty=1$ for all $n$ (bounded), but $F$ is \emph{not} equicontinuous at $x=1$:
	for any $\delta>0$, choose $n$ so large that $(1-\delta)^n<\tfrac12$, hence
	\[
	|f_n(1)-f_n(1-\delta)|=1-(1-\delta)^n>\tfrac12.
	\]
	Moreover $f_n\to \mathbf 1_{\{1\}}$ pointwise, so no subsequence can converge uniformly in $C([0,1])$.
	Thus boundedness alone does not give relative compactness.

	\medskip
	\textbf{(2) Equicontinuous but unbounded $\Rightarrow$ not relatively compact.}
	Let $X=[0,1]$ and $F=\{\,c_n:\ c_n(x)\equiv n,\ n\in\mathbb N\,\}$.
	Each $c_n$ is constant, hence $F$ is equicontinuous, but $\|c_n\|_\infty=n\to\infty$,
	so $F$ is not bounded and certainly not totally bounded (hence not relatively compact).

	\medskip
	\textbf{(3) Bounded \& equicontinuous, but $X$ noncompact $\Rightarrow$ failure.}
	Let $X=\mathbb R$ and fix $\phi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$ with $\|\phi\|_\infty=1$ and
	$\operatorname{supp}\phi\subset[-1,1]$. Consider
	\[
	F=\{\,\phi(\,\cdot - t\,):\ t\in\mathbb R\,\}.
	\]
	Then $F$ is uniformly bounded and equicontinuous (derivatives are uniformly bounded),
	but $F$ is \emph{not} totally bounded in $\|\cdot\|_\infty$:
	if $|t-s|>3$ then $\operatorname{supp}(\phi(\cdot-t))\cap\operatorname{supp}(\phi(\cdot-s))=\varnothing$,
	hence $\|\phi(\cdot-t)-\phi(\cdot-s)\|_\infty=1$. No finite $\varepsilon$--net exists for $\varepsilon<1$.
	Thus compactness of the domain $X$ is crucial.

	\medskip
	\textbf{(4) Closed and bounded need not be equicontinuous (hence not compact).}
	The unit ball of $C([0,1])$,
	\[
	B=\{\,f\in C([0,1]): \|f\|_\infty\le 1\,\},
	\]
	is closed and bounded but \emph{not} equicontinuous: for any $\delta>0$ one can build a continuous
	``spike'' $s_\delta$ of height $1$ supported in an interval of width $<\delta$.
	Hence $B$ is not compact (reflecting infinite dimensionality).

	\medskip
	\textbf{Why equicontinuity matters (Arzel\`a--Ascoli).}
	If $X$ is compact and $F\subset C(X)$ is bounded and equicontinuous, then $F$ is totally bounded in
	$(C(X),\|\cdot\|_\infty)$; the closure $\overline F$ is compact. Conversely, if $F$ is totally bounded,
	then $F$ is bounded and equicontinuous. Thus, on compact $X$,
	\[
	\boxed{\text{$F$ compact } \Longleftrightarrow\ \text{$F$ closed, bounded, and equicontinuous}.}
	\]

	\medskip
	\textbf{Proof sketch: totally bounded $\Rightarrow$ equicontinuous.}
	Fix $\varepsilon>0$. Choose a finite $\varepsilon/3$--net $\{g_1,\dots,g_m\}\subset F$ in $\|\cdot\|_\infty$.
	Each $g_i$ is uniformly continuous on compact $X$; pick $\delta>0$ working for all $i$.
	For $d(x,y)<\delta$ and $f\in F$, pick $i$ with $\|f-g_i\|_\infty<\varepsilon/3$:
	\[
	|f(x)-f(y)|\le 2\|f-g_i\|_\infty+|g_i(x)-g_i(y)|<\varepsilon.
	\]
	This $\delta$ is independent of $f$ and $x$, so $F$ is equicontinuous.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Proof sketch: bounded \& equicontinuous $\Rightarrow$ totally bounded.}
	Let $\varepsilon>0$. Equicontinuity gives $\delta>0$ with
	$d(x,y)<\delta \Rightarrow |f(x)-f(y)|<\varepsilon/3$ for all $f\in F$.
	Cover compact $X$ by finitely many balls $B(x_j,\delta)$, $j=1,\dots,N$.
	Boundedness gives $|f|\le M$ on $X$ for all $f\in F$; partition $[-M,M]$ into intervals of length $\varepsilon/3$.
	For each $f\in F$, choose grid points $a_j$ with $|f(x_j)-a_j|\le \varepsilon/6$ and define a template function that on
	$B(x_j,\delta)$ takes value $a_j$. This finite template family is an $\varepsilon$--net of $F$, hence $F$ is totally bounded.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Takeaway.}
	\emph{Equicontinuity} provides a uniform modulus of continuity for the family and prevents arbitrarily
	narrow spikes; combined with boundedness and compactness of the domain, it yields total boundedness
	(and thus compactness) in the sup norm via Arzel\`a--Ascoli.

	\textbf{Relative compactness (precompactness).}

	Let $A\subset X$ where $X$ is a topological (or metric) space.
	We say $A$ is \emph{relatively compact} (or \emph{precompact}) if $\overline{A}$ is compact in $X$.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Metric characterizations.}
	For $A$ in a metric space:
	\begin{center}
	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
	A \text{ is relatively compact }
	\iff \text{ every sequence in } A \text{ has a convergent subsequence in } X
	\iff A \text{ is totally bounded.}
	$}
	\end{center}
	If $X$ is complete, then $\overline{A}$ compact $\iff$ $A$ totally bounded.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Examples.}
	In $\mathbb{R}^n$, $A$ relatively compact $\iff$ $A$ bounded (Heine--Borel).
	In $C([0,1])$ with $\|\cdot\|_\infty$, bounded \& equicontinuous families are relatively compact (Arzel\`a--Ascoli),
	but the unit ball is not relatively compact.

{\large\bfseries Totally bounded sets: definition, examples, and counterexamples.\par}

	\medskip
	\textbf{Definition.}
	A subset $A$ of a metric space $(X,d)$ is \emph{totally bounded} if
	for every $\varepsilon>0$ there exist points $x_1,\dots,x_N\in X$
	such that
	\[
	A \subset \bigcup_{i=1}^N B(x_i,\varepsilon).
	\]
	Equivalently, $A$ admits a finite $\varepsilon$--net for each $\varepsilon>0$.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Characterizations.}

		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad $A$ is totally bounded $\iff$ every sequence in $A$ has a Cauchy subsequence.
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad If $X$ is complete, then $A$ is totally bounded $\iff$ $\overline{A}$ is compact (i.e., $A$ is relatively compact).
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Always: totally bounded $\Rightarrow$ bounded (the converse may fail).

	\medskip
	\textbf{Finite vs.\ infinite dimension.}

		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad In $\mathbb{R}^n$ (any finite--dimensional normed space), bounded $\iff$ totally bounded (Heine--Borel).
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad In infinite--dimensional normed spaces, there exist bounded sets that are not totally bounded.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Examples (totally bounded).}

		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad $[a,b]\subset\mathbb{R}$ and, more generally, any bounded subset of $\mathbb{R}^n$.
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad $\mathbb{Q}\cap[0,1]$ in the Euclidean metric (subset of a totally bounded set).
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Any compact set in a metric space.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Counterexamples (not totally bounded).}

		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad $\mathbb{R}$ or $\mathbb{N}$ in the usual metric (unbounded).
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad $(\mathbb{R},d)$ with $d(x,y)=\min\{1,|x-y|\}$:
		the space is bounded (diameter $1$) but not totally bounded, since for $\varepsilon<1$
		one needs infinitely many $\varepsilon$-balls to cover $\mathbb{R}$.
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad The unit ball of an infinite--dimensional Banach space (e.g., $\ell^2$):
		bounded but not totally bounded; the orthonormal vectors $\{e_k\}$ remain $\sqrt2$ apart.
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Any infinite discrete metric space $(X,d)$ with $d(x,y)=1$ for $x\neq y$:
		bounded but not totally bounded, because for $\varepsilon<1$
		each $\varepsilon$-ball is a singleton.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Basic properties.}

		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Subsets, finite unions, and continuous images of totally bounded sets are totally bounded.
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Products of totally bounded sets are totally bounded (for the product metric).

	{\Large\bfseries $\varepsilon$-nets: finite vs.\ infinite (definition, theory, examples).\par}

	\medskip
	\textbf{Definition.}
	Let $(X,d)$ be a metric space and $A\subseteq X$. For $\varepsilon>0$ a set $N\subseteq X$ is an \emph{$\varepsilon$-net for $A$} if
	\[
	A \subseteq \bigcup_{x\in N} B(x,\varepsilon).
	\]
	Equivalently, for every $a\in A$ there exists $x\in N$ with $d(a,x)<\varepsilon$.
	If such $N$ can be chosen finite, we say $A$ admits a \emph{finite $\varepsilon$-net}.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Covering and packing numbers.}
	The \emph{covering number} $N(A,\varepsilon)$ is the minimal size of a finite $\varepsilon$-net for $A$ (or $+\infty$ if none exists).
	An \emph{$\varepsilon$-packing} is a set $P\subseteq A$ with the balls $\{B(p,\varepsilon)\}_{p\in P}$ pairwise disjoint;
	its maximal size is the \emph{packing number} $M(A,\varepsilon)$.
	Standard bounds:
	\[
	M(A,2\varepsilon)\ \le\ N(A,\varepsilon)\ \le\ M(A,\varepsilon).
	\]

	\medskip
	\textbf{Total boundedness.}
	A set $A$ is \emph{totally bounded} iff $N(A,\varepsilon)<\infty$ for every $\varepsilon>0$.
	In a complete metric space, $A$ is totally bounded $\iff$ $\overline{A}$ is compact (i.e., $A$ is relatively compact).

	\medskip
	\textbf{Separability and countable $\varepsilon$-nets.}
	If $X$ is separable with countable dense set $D$, then $D$ is an $\varepsilon$-net for every $\varepsilon>0$ (since $B(x,\varepsilon)\cap D\neq\varnothing$ for all $x$).
	Thus countable $\varepsilon$-nets always exist in separable spaces, though they need not be finite.

	\medskip
	\textbf{Examples (finite $\varepsilon$-nets exist).}

		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad $[0,1]\subset\mathbb R$ with the Euclidean metric. A grid $\{0,\varepsilon,2\varepsilon,\dots,1\}$ is a finite $\varepsilon$-net; $N([0,1],\varepsilon)\sim \lceil 1/\varepsilon\rceil$.
		\par\noindent\textbullet\quad Any bounded subset of $\mathbb R^n$. Cubic lattices produce finite $\varepsilon$-nets; hence bounded $\iff$ totally bounded in finite dimensions.
